Verifiers and Generators: Epistemic Semantics for Intuitionistic Logic (Long Version)
Pablo Barenbaum

Abstract
. This paper explores epistemic realizability , a form of realizability in which the property that a piece of data constitutes evidence for a logical proposition is semi-decidable. In this framework, each proposition A A is assigned a verifier program ⟨ A ⟩ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle} that checks whether a datum X X is a realizer for A A , and a dual generator program ✠ A {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A} that behaves as a generic realizer for A A . We propose epistemic realizability interpretations for minimal logic, second-order intuitionistic logic, and higher-order intuitionistic logic, proving that each system is sound and complete under the proposed semantics.



1.
Introduction

Verificationism explains the meaning of logical connectives by means
of the notion of
evidence
rather than the notion of
truth
.
Under this view,
the meaning of a proposition
A
is given by a
verification procedure

to recognize evidence for
A

(
15
)
.
This work explores the problem of providing a formal semantics for logical
connectives in an “epistemically acceptable” way.
Formally, this means that verification procedures must be
effectively checkable
:
the meaning of a proposition
A
must be a program
P

that computes a
partial computable predicate
,
such that
P
accepts
X
if it constitutes evidence
for
A
, and either rejects
X
or remains undefined otherwise.
We call this “epistemic semantics”, following Nelson
(
37
, Sec. 6)
.

The primary challenge lies in providing
epistemic semantics for logical
connectives
. Standard semantic frameworks typically do not provide effective
means of recognizing evidence.
For example, classical model-theoretic semantics defines the meaning of
a universal quantifier
\forall x.P(x)
by declaring that a structure satisfies the formula
if and only if it satisfies
P(a)
for every element
a
in the universe.
While this definition is mathematically acceptable
—assuming a prior understanding of universal quantification in the metalanguage—
it is unsatisfactory from the perspective of epistemic semantics.
Rather than providing an effective verification procedure
to determine whether an object is evidence for
\forall x.P(x)
,
the meaning of the universal quantifier is displaced from the object language to the metatheory.

Realizability interpretations.

Many constructive approaches to semantics take
BHK-style
1
1

1

BHK stands for Brouwer–Heyting–Kolmogorov.

interpretations as their starting point
(
52
)
.
These interpretations align with verificationism
by defining the meaning of a proposition
through its
realizers
(also referred to as
constructions
,

proofs
,
evidence
, etc.).
One formal incarnation of these ideas
is Kleene’s realizability
(
29
)
,
in which the meaning of conjunction is defined as:

\langle n,m\rangle\Vdash(A\land B)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{\iff}}\,n\Vdash A\text{ and }m\Vdash B

where
\langle n,m\rangle
represents Gödel’s pairing function
and
n\Vdash A
denotes that the natural number
n
is a realizer for
A
.
This definition declares that a realizer for a conjunction
A\land B

is given by a pair containing a realizer for
A
and a realizer for
B
.
Moreover, it is
epistemically acceptable
, in the sense that it provides
a decision procedure: to verify that
a pair
\langle n,m\rangle
is a realizer for
A\land B
, one can recursively verify
that
n
is a realizer for
A
and
m
is a realizer for
B
.

Troublesome connectives.

Unfortunately, BHK-style interpretations do not provide epistemically acceptable
definitions for all connectives. In particular, the semantics of “troublesome”
operators —implication, negation, universal quantification— is
expressed in terms of
meta-level
universal quantification.
For instance, the meaning of implication in Kleene’s realizability
is defined as follows:

n\Vdash(A\Rightarrow B)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{\iff}}\,\forall m\in\mathbb{N}.\,(m\Vdash A\implies\varphi_{n}(m)\Vdash B)

where
\varphi_{n}
denotes the
n
-th partial computable function.
This definition declares that a realizer for an implication
A\Rightarrow B

is given by a partial computable function
\varphi_{n}

that transforms
every
realizer for
A
into a realizer for
B
.
This is a priori
not epistemically acceptable
, because
to verify that
n
is a realizer for
A\Rightarrow B
, one would have to
semi-decide a universal quantification
\forall m\in\mathbb{N}.(...)

over the natural numbers, which is in general an impossible task
2
2

2

The meaning of implication in the object language depends
on the meaning of universal quantification in the metatheory.
Note that there are ways to understand the universal formula

\psi\equiv\forall m\in\mathbb{N}.(...)
in an epistemically acceptable way;
for example, as the existence
of a derivation of
\psi
in Peano arithmetic. (See the subsequent comments on formalism).

.

The formalist escape route.

As discussed above, the intuitive “meaning” of certain connectives,
specifically
implication
,
negation
, and
universal quantification
,
seems difficult to reconcile with an epistemic semantics.
One way to achieve this reconciliation is through
formalism
.
Formalism can be synthesized as the view that evidence for a proposition
A
is
provided by a derivation of
A
in a specific formal proof system.
Any proof system that has the property of
semi-decidable proof-checking

immediately provides a trivial epistemic semantics for propositions.
Indeed, the verification procedure is given by the proof-checker itself,
and the string of symbols encoding the derivation serves as the required
existential witness.
Formalism allows us to understand mathematical activity as a
combinatorial game of producing derivations, but leaves open the question
of whether propositions can be given any other meaning beyond the symbols
themselves. For example, in the absence of semantics, proof systems appear
as entirely arbitrary constructions, and it is impossible to even wonder
whether they are sound or complete.

Epistemic realizability.

This work studies a
epistemic realizability
semantics
for logical propositions, inspired by BHK-style
interpretations and traditional realizability,
but formulated from the perspective of epistemic semantics.
Early works on realizability were formulated in the language of arithmetic,
so data types other than integers had to be handled through explicit
Gödel codings.
In this paper we use
—as other works also do—
extensions of the
untyped
\lambda
-calculus

as the language
to express both programs and data
3
3

3

This choice is not
essential but quite convenient.
.

The interpretations we propose
center on the dual notions of
verifiers
and
generators
.
We begin by interpreting each proposition
A
as a

program
called its
verifier
and written
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
.
The verifier for
A
is an untyped
\lambda
-term
that computes a partial computable predicate.
We say that
X
is a
realizer
for
A

if, by definition,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
succeeds.
Success, in this framework, is defined by the property that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
where
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}
denotes reduction in zero or more steps
and
\star
is a constant representing successful termination.
Note that the input
X
is itself expressed using an untyped

\lambda
-term.

For example, the meaning of a conjunction
A\land B

is a program
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\land B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}

such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\land B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
succeeds
if both
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\pi_{1}(X))
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\pi_{2}(X))

succeed, where
\pi_{1}(X)
and
\pi_{2}(X)
are the first and second
projections of a pair, in accordance with the BHK interpretation.

Verifiers and generators.

A significant difficulty arises when attempting to define the meaning
of “troublesome” connectives. For example, implication is challenging
due to the reversal of
polarity
in the antecedent.
According to the BHK interpretation,
the verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
must take an input
X

and determine whether, for every
Y
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(Y)

succeeds, the application
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X\,Y)
also succeeds. This verification
is not decidable a priori. Indeed, the predicate:

P(X)\,\,\,\,\equiv\,\,\,\,\forall Y.({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(Y)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X\,Y)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star)

starts with a universal quantifier. This is not a priori semi-decidable,
so it is unclear how to define a program
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
that computes
P
.

To resolve this, we refine the interpretation: now each proposition
A

is interpreted by both its
verifier

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}

and a second program, called its
generator
and written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
.
The generator for
A
is an untyped
\lambda
-term that
behaves as a generic realizer for
A
.
For example, in the case of conjunction,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\land B}
behaves as the pair
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})
,
also in accordance with the BHK interpretation.

From a computer science perspective,
a verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
is a program that
tests

whether its argument complies with a specification
A
.
Conversely, a generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}{}
serves as a
stub
,
or a “mock implementation” that behaves as if it were compliant
with said specification.
One basic property of this system is
correctness
:
a verifier must accept its corresponding generator as a valid realizer,
that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Aided by the presence of generators, we can define the verifier
for an implication in such a way that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
succeeds if and only if

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})
succeeds.
That is, to determine whether
X
is a realizer for
A\Rightarrow B
,
we check whether
X
produces a realizer for
B

when supplied with the realizer for
A
produced by the generator.

To complete the picture, we need to define the
generator for an implication
A\Rightarrow B
.
Verifiers and generators are defined in a mutually inductive fashion.
Indeed, the generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}

is a program that takes an arbitrary input
X

and returns
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}
provided that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
succeeds.
Formally, this can be written as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}=\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})
.
Here
\lambda x.\,(...)
is the standard
\lambda
-notation for anonymous functions,
and
M{?}N
is an operator that proceeds to evaluate
N

provided that the evaluation of
M
terminates successfully.

Scope of the work and propositions-as-types.

We study epistemic realizability across three incrementally more
expressive systems of intuitionistic logic:
1.
minimal logic
;
2.
second-order intuitionistic logic
;
and 3.
higher-order intuitionistic logic
.
As is well-known, derivations of sequents in natural deduction systems
correspond to terms in typed
\lambda
-calculi, through the propositions-as-types
correspondence (
cf.
for instance
(
22
;
49
)
).
The formal deductive systems that we study are three standard typed

\lambda
-calculi corresponding to each of the logics above, namely:
1.
simply typed
\lambda
-calculus
(
{\lambda}^{\to}
);
2.
polymorphic
\lambda
-calculus
(System F
(
23
;
45
)
); and
3.
higher-order polymorphic
\lambda
-calculus
(System F
\omega

(
23
)
).

Contributions.

For each of the three aforementioned typed
\lambda
-calculi,
we define a corresponding
untyped

\lambda
-calculus,
called the
metacalculus
.
For example, for the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus (
{\lambda}^{\to}
)
we define a metacalculus (
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
)
that allows to express both verifiers
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
and generators
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}

for every proposition
A
in the language of minimal logic.
For each system, the main result is that the logic is
both
sound
and
complete
with respect to the epistemic realizability
interpretation. This establishes that
A
is a theorem in the
logical theory if and only if there exists evidence for
A
in the metacalculus,
that is, an untyped term
X
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
succeeds.
For completeness to hold, we further require that the evidence
X
must
be a
normalizing
and
pure
term; that is, it must have a
normal form and not contain extraneous elements such as generators
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
.

While relating typed
\lambda
-calculi and epistemic realizability
may appear straightforward, proving soundness and completeness presents
significant technical challenges.
For example, proving soundness of the
modus ponens
rule,
requires showing that if both
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(Y)

succeed then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X\,Y)
must also succeed.
In traditional realizability, this is precisely the definition
of implication, leaving nothing to prove.
In our “epistemic” setting, however,
the condition that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X)
succeeds
only guarantees that
X
behaves correctly when applied to the
generator

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
. Consequently, one must prove that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}Y\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

imply
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(X\,Y)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Establishing this property is a non-trivial task, as it requires to show
that in some sense
X
cannot distinguish between the actual realizer
Y

and the “mock” realizer produced by the generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
.

Structure of the paper.

In
section

2
we review some preliminary notions and fix
some of the notations used in the rest of the paper.
In
section

3
we study epistemic realizability for
minimal logic.
In
section

4
we extend the results to second-order logic.
This requires to generalize the notions of verifier and generator to deal
with second-order universal quantifiers. The proof techniques also have
to be adapted, to address the usual difficulties posed by impredicativity.
In
section

5
we discuss the extension to higher-order logic.
Finally, in
section

6
we conclude,
and discuss some potential applications in programming languages and
proof assistants, as well as related and future work.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we recall some standard notions, to fix nomenclature and
notation.

We work with various typed and untyped
\lambda
-calculi,
all of them instances of
abstract rewriting systems
(ARSs).
An ARS is a pair
(X,\to)
, where
X
is a set of elements called
objects
,
and
\to
is a binary relation
{\to}\subseteq X\times X
.
Standard references are
(
2
;
50
)
for rewriting systems
and
(
4
;
3
)
for the
\lambda
-calculus.
If
R\subseteq X\times Y
and
S\subseteq Y\times Z
are binary relations,
we write
R\circ S
(or just
R\,S
) for their composition, defined by

\{(x,z)\in X\times Z\ |\ \exists y\in Y.\,x\mathrel{R}y\land y\mathrel{R}z\}
.
If
(X,\to)
is an ARS, we write
\to^{0}
for the diagonal relation
and
{\to^{n+1}}\,\mathrel{:=}\,(\to\circ\to^{n})
.
We write
\to^{=}
for the reflexive closure
(
i.e.

{\to^{=}}={\to^{0}}\cup{\to}
);

\to^{-1}
or
\leftarrow
for the inverse relation
(
i.e.

{\leftarrow}=\{(y,x)\ |\ x\to y\}
);

\to^{*}
or
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}
for the reflexive–transitive closure
(
i.e.

{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}=\cup_{n\geq 0}\to^{n}
);

\leftrightarrow
for the symmetric closure
(
i.e.

{\leftrightarrow}={\leftarrow}\cup{\to}
);
and
\twoheadleftarrow\!\!\!\twoheadrightarrow
for reflexive–symmetric–transitive closure
(
i.e.

{\twoheadleftarrow\!\!\!\twoheadrightarrow}={\leftrightarrow^{*}}
).
Given a relation
\to_{X}
, we write
=_{X}
for
\twoheadleftarrow\!\!\!\twoheadrightarrow_{X}
,
and call it
X
-convertibility
.
Given relations
\to_{X}
and
\to_{Y}
,
we write
\to_{XY}
for their union.

An object
x
is
\to
-
irreducible
or a
\to
-
normal form

if there is no
y
such that
x\to y
.
An object
x
is
\to
-
normalizing
if there exists a normal form
y

such that
x\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}y
.
An ARS is
deterministic

if for all objects
x,y,y^{\prime}
such that
x\to y
and
x\to y^{\prime}

one has that
y=y^{\prime}
.
An ARS has the
diamond property
if
{\leftarrow\,\to}\subseteq{\to\,\leftarrow}
,
it is
subcommutative
if
{\leftarrow\,\to}\subseteq{\to^{=}\,\leftarrow^{=}}
,
and it is
confluent
or
Church–Rosser

if
{\twoheadleftarrow\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\,\twoheadleftarrow}
.
Determinism, the diamond property, and subcommutativity are
each stronger properties than confluence.
The relations
\to_{1}
and
\to_{2}

commute
if

{\twoheadleftarrow_{1}\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{2}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{2}\,\twoheadleftarrow_{1}}
.

In the concrete rewriting systems that we use, objects are
expressions

(
M,N,A,B,t,s,\ldots
) of various sorts
(terms, types, etc.).
Expressions may contain occurrences of
variables
(
x,y,a,b,\ldots
)
of various sorts (variables, type-variables, etc.).
Some syntactic constructors may bind variables,
e.g.
a
\lambda
-abstraction
\lambda x.\,t
binds the occurrences of
x
in

t
. The notions of free and bound occurrences of a variable are defined
as expected in each case. We work implicitly up to
\alpha
-renaming of bound variables.
We write
\mathsf{fv}(E)
to denote the set of free variables of the
expression
E
. We write
E_{1}\{x:=E_{2}\}
for the expression that results
from performing the capture-avoiding substitution of all the free occurrences
of
x
in
E_{1}
by
E_{2}
.
Sometimes we work with substitutions
\sigma
, which are functions mapping
variables to expressions.
We write
\sigma[x\mapsto E]
for the extension of the substitution
\sigma

with the mapping
x\mapsto E
,
i.e.
the substitution
\sigma^{\prime}
such that

\sigma^{\prime}(y)=E
if
x=y
,
and
\sigma^{\prime}(y)=\sigma(y)
otherwise.
We write
E^{\sigma}
to denote the expression
that results from the parallel substitution of each free occurrence of each
free variable
x
in
E
by
\sigma(x)
, avoiding capture.
The usual properties of substitution apply in these cases,

e.g.

E_{1}\{x:=E_{2}\}^{\sigma}=E_{1}^{\sigma}\{x:=E_{2}^{\sigma}\}

holds assuming that
x
is
safe
for
\sigma
,

i.e.

x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\sigma(y))
for every
y
.
Sometimes we use these standard syntactical facts without providing
explicit proofs.

In these systems we usually consider
contexts
,
which are expressions with a single occurrence of a distinguished free
variable
\Box
, called the
hole
.
If
\mathtt{C}
is a context and
E
is an expression, we write

\mathtt{C}[E]
for the expression that results from “plugging”
E

into the hole of
\mathtt{C}
, possibly capturing variables.

3.
Epistemic Realizability for Minimal Logic

In this section, we develop a epistemic realizability semantics
for
minimal logic
, the intuitionistic system
that includes implication as the single connective.
The main results in this section are
soundness
(
theorem

3.11
)
and
completeness
(
theorem

3.14
).

The simply typed
\lambda
-calculus (
{\lambda}^{\to}
).

We begin by recalling the typed
\lambda
-calculus that corresponds
to minimal logic from the perspective of the propositions-as-types correspondence,
the (Curry-style) simply typed
\lambda
-calculus.
We assume given a denumerable set of
atomic types
(
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\ldots
)
and
variables
(
x,y,\ldots
).
The sets of
types
and
terms
of
{\lambda}^{\to}
are given by:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\textsc{Types }&A,B,\ldots&::=&\mathbf{a}\mid A\Rightarrow B\\
\textsc{Terms}&t,s,\ldots&::=&x\mid\lambda x.\,t\mid t\,s\end{array}

An
environment
, ranged over by
\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
,
is a finite set of type assignments
\{x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\}
,
where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are distinct variables.
We write
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
for the set of variables that appear in
\Gamma
.

Typing judgements
are of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:A
,
and defined inductively by the following rules:

\frac{}{\Gamma,x:A\vdash x:A}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{\Gamma,x:A\vdash t:B}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,t:A\Rightarrow B}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:A\Rightarrow B\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash s:A}{\Gamma\vdash t\,s:B}{\texttt{}}

The binary relations of
\beta
and
\eta
-reduction
are defined by the following reduction rules, closed under arbitrary contexts:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l@{\HS\HS}r@{\,}c@{\,}ll}(\lambda x.\,t)\,s&\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}&t\{x:=s\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt&\lambda x.\,t\,x&\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}&t&\text{if $x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}\\
\end{array}

Both notions of reduction are defined over untyped terms,
but they can be restricted to typable terms, using the well-known
property that they preserve types (
i.e.

subject reduction
holds).

The metacalculus (
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
).

Next, we define the
metacalculus

in which we will be able to formulate the notions of verifiers, generators,
realizers, and so on, for minimal logic.
The metacalculus
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an extension of the
untyped

\lambda
-calculus.
It would be possible to formulate the semantics using the (unextended)

\lambda
-calculus directly, but working in an extension is
clearer, and more convenient, as it avoids the accidental complexities
of encodings.

To emphasize their specific role, and to avoid confusion
with
{\lambda}^{\to}
-terms, terms of the metacalculus are called
metaterms
.
The set of
metaterms
of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is given by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{llrll}\textsc{Metaterms }&M,N,\ldots&::=&x&\text{variable}\\
&&\mid&\lambda x.\,M&\text{abstraction}\\
&&\mid&M\,N&\text{application}\\
&&\mid&\star&\text{success }\\
&&\mid&M{?}N&\text{guard }\\
&&\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}&\text{generator}\\
&&\mid&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\text{verifier}\end{array}

For every atomic type
\mathbf{a}

there are metaterms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
.
In the case of an arbitrary type
A
,
the metaterms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M

shall be defined below (
definition

3.1
).

We write
\mathtt{W},\mathtt{W}^{\prime},\ldots
to stand for the set of
weak head contexts
,
defined by the following grammar:

\mathtt{W}::=\Box\mid\mathtt{W}\,M\mid\mathtt{W}{?}M\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\mathtt{W}

We define
reduction
in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, written
\to
, as the union
of the three reduction rules below, closed by arbitrary contexts.
Moreover,
weak head reduction
, written
\to_{w}
,
is defined as the union of the same three reduction rules,
but closed by weak head contexts.

\begin{array}[]{rll}(\lambda x.\,M)\,N&\to&M\{x:=N\}\\
\star{?}M&\to&M\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}&\to&\star\end{array}

We use the constant
\star
to represent that a computation
succeeds
.
The guard
M{?}N
can be used to check whether
M

succeeds and return
N
if this is the case.
A metaterm of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}

represents a generic realizer for the atomic type
\mathbf{a}
,
while a metaterm of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
intuitively “checks”
whether
M
is a realizer for the atomic type
\mathbf{a}
.

We adopt the convention that the guard has higher precedence than
the abstraction, but lower precedence than the application and
the verifier, so for example

\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M{?}N\,P

is equivalent to

\lambda x.\,(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M){?}(N\,P))
.

A metaterm is
pure
if it only involves variables,
abstractions, and applications.
Terms of
{\lambda}^{\to}
are identified with pure metaterms of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Note that reduction in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
over pure terms is just
standard
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
-reduction.
A metaterm is
good
if it is pure and
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
-normalizing.

Interpretation of types.

The metacalculus
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
includes generators and verifiers

only for atomic types
.
We extend them to arbitrary types
A

by providing mutually recursive definitions of the generator

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
that produces a generic realizer of
A
,
and the verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
that checks whether
its argument
M
is a realizer for
A
.

Definition 3.1 (Generators and verifiers for
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
).

Let
A
be a type.
The
generator
for
A
, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
,
and the
verifier
for
A
with argument
M
, written
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
,
are metaterms defined as follows by induction on
A
.
In the base case, when
A=\mathbf{a}
is an atomic type,
the definition coincides with the metaterm constructors in the grammar
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
).
In the recursive case:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}&\,\mathrel{:=}&\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\,\mathrel{:=}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})\end{array}

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}
intuitively means that,
to generate a generic realizer for
A\Rightarrow B
,
we may construct an
abstraction
\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})

that checks whether its argument is a realizer for
A
,
and, if this succeeds, generates a realizer for
B
.
The definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
intuitively means that,
to verify whether
M
is a realizer for
A\Rightarrow B
,
we provide
M
with a generic realizer for
A
, and check whether the
result is a realizer for
B
.

Formally, we say that
M
is a
realizer
for
A

if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
, and we say that
A
is

realizable
if it has a good realizer.

Note on syntax.

The syntax for verifiers requires
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
to always
be accompanied by its argument
M
. This is just a syntactic
constraint that helps simplify some proofs.
If the verifier needs to be treated as a stand-alone operator
it can be defined via an
\eta
-like expansion
as
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x
.

Example 3.2 (The
K
and
S
combinators realize Hilbert-style axioms).

The following reduction shows that the
K
combinator
(
\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x
)
realizes the first axiom (
\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}
)
in the Hilbert-style system
for minimal logic:

\begin{array}[]{lll}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x)&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}})\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\\
&\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\to\star\end{array}

The
S
combinator
(
\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\lambda z.\,x\,z\,(y\,z)
),
in turn, realizes the second axiom

((\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c})\Rightarrow(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b})\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c})
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c})\Rightarrow(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b})\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\lambda z.\,x\,z\,(y\,z))\\
=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\lambda z.\,x\,z\,(y\,z))\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}})\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}))\\
=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}))\\
\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}})\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}))\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}}\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}))\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{b}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{c}})({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}))\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{b}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}){?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{c}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\ldots\to\star\end{array}

The two following examples illustrate applications of epistemic realizability.
Note that these are well-known results that can be established through other
methods, such as standard realizability interpretations.

Example 3.3 (Consistency).

It straightforward to show that
not every type is realizable
.
Indeed, let
\mathbf{a}
be an arbitrary atomic type and suppose that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
for a pure
metaterm
M
.
Inspecting the rewriting rules of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
it is clear that this could only be possible if there were a reduction

M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
. But
M
is pure and contains no generators,
and it is straighforward to show that reduction cannot create generators,
so we obtain a contradiction.
Combining this with soundness (
theorem

3.11
), we conclude that not
every formula is a theorem in minimal logic; that is, the logic is
consistent
.

Example 3.4 (Specification results).

The interpretation can be used to characterize the inherent
computational behaviour of the realizers of a given type.
For example, we claim that if
M
is a pure term that realizes
\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}
,
then
M\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\lambda x.\,x
.
To see this (informally), suppose that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
By definition, we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
This can only happen if
M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
.
But
M
is a pure term without verifiers, and reduction cannot spontaneously
create a verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
. So
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
can be understood as
just an “inert” symbol that does not interact with
M
,
and it can be replaced by a fresh variable
x

to obtain a reduction
M\,x\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}x
.
Since reduction in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
over pure terms
is
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
-reduction, it follows that
M\,x\mathrel{=_{\beta}}x
,
which is easily seen to imply
M\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\lambda x.\,x
.
Combining this with soundness (
theorem

3.11
), one obtains that

the unique realizer of
\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}
is the identity
.
This is a classic result, dating back at least to Reynolds
(
44
)
.

Rewriting properties of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Before going on, we mention some basic syntactic results that are necessary
to show that the interpretation is sound and complete.
First, it is straightforward to show that there is at most
one weak head redex (see
section

A.1
). Hence:

Proposition 3.5.

Weak head reduction
\to_{w}
is deterministic.

Moreover,
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an

orthogonal higher-order rewriting system
in the sense of
Nipkow
(
38
)
, which entails:

Proposition 3.6.

Reduction in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is confluent.

A further rewriting property that we need is that
reductions ending in
\star
can be
standardized

to weak head reductions.
Compare this property with the well-known fact that the head
reduction strategy is head normalizing in the

\lambda
-calculus (see
e.g.

(
4
, Coro. 11.4.8)
).
We do not include an explicit proof of this result, but
section

4

proves an analogous fact for the (more difficult) second-order case:

Proposition 3.7 (Standardization).

If
M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
then
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

3.1.
Soundness of
{\lambda}^{\to}
with respect to
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}

Our goal is to show that the typed calculus
{\lambda}^{\to}
is sound
and complete with respect to the verification semantics induced
by the metacalculus
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
In this section we focus on
soundness
, which states that
all theorems of minimal logic are realizable.
More precisely, whenever
t
is a
proof
of
A
,

i.e.
a closed term
t
such that
\vdash t:A
holds,
then
t
is also a
realizer
4
4

4

Recall that all terms of the typed calculus
{\lambda}^{\to}

can be understood as pure metaterms of the metacalculus
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

for
A
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Moreover,
t
is clearly
good
:
it is pure by construction, and the standard fact that simply typed
terms are strongly normalizing implies that
t
is
\beta
-normalizing.

Stating soundness for open terms.

To be able to reason inductively over the structure of terms,
we are compelled to generalize the statement of soundness
to
open
terms,
i.e.
terms that contain free variables.
The generalized version states that if

x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash t:B

holds, then to obtain a realizer for
B

one should replace each variable
x_{i}
by the generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{i}}
.
Formally, one has that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}}\}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Substitutions and generative substitutions.

A
substitution
(
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\ldots
)
is a function mapping variables to metaterms.
Recall that we write
M^{\sigma}
for the result
of applying the substition
\sigma
on each free variable of
M
,
avoiding capture.

The
generative substitution
for an environment
\Gamma

is written
\underline{\Gamma}

and defined as the substitution
such that
\underline{\Gamma}(x)={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
holds for every
x:A\in\Gamma
,
and
\underline{\Gamma}(x)=x
holds for every
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.
By abuse of notation, sometimes we write just
\Gamma
for the generative
substitution, rather than
\underline{\Gamma}
.
For example, if
\Gamma=(x:A,y:B)
then:

((\lambda x.\,x\,y\,y)\,x)^{\Gamma}=(\lambda x.\,x\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}

Using generative substitutions, soundness
can be refined to state that
\Gamma\vdash t:B

implies
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Correctness and universality.

The proofs of soundness and completeness that we provide in this section
are “syntactic” in nature, as they rely only on rewriting techniques and
elementary structural induction over the size of expressions.
We anticipate that the transition to second and higher-order logic in
the following sections shall require a different approach.

The proof of soundness of minimal logic relies on the two following
key properties:

1.

Correctness.

The generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
is accepted by the verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
,
that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

2.

Universality.

The generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
is “universal”
with respect to the verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
.
More precisely, if
P
and
M
are metaterms such that
P
accepts the generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
,
and the verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
accepts
M
,
then
P
accepts
M
.
Schematically:

\frac{P\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star}{P\,M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star}{\texttt{}}

These two conditions together state that verifier/generator
pairs enjoy a sort of ‘‘universal property’’
5
5

5

It could be interesting to understand verifier/generator pairs from a categorical perspective.

.
Correctness is easy to show:

Lemma 3.8 (Correctness).

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

Proof.

By induction on
A
.
If
A=\mathbf{a}

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\to\star
.
If
A=(B\Rightarrow C)
, then:

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\,\,}llll}&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\Rightarrow C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\Rightarrow C}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\to C}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\\
&\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\text{i.h. }}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\star{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\text{i.h. }}\star\qed\end{array}

The property of universality is comparatively much subtler.
We first observe that if
P\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
P
is
\lambda
-abstraction that consumes its argument,
for example
P=\lambda x.\,P^{\prime}
,
then
P\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\to P^{\prime}\{x:={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Indeed, to reason inductively we need to consider the following generalization
of the statement of universality, in which occurrences of generators

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}}
inside a term may be replaced by
terms
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
that are accepted by the corresponding verifiers.
The property of universality becomes:

\frac{P\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}}\}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star\hskip 14.22636pt({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M_{i}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star)_{1\leq i\leq n}}{P\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:=M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star}{\texttt{}}

The first premise of this “rule” can be rephrased,
using a generative substitution, as

P^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
assuming that
\Gamma
is the environment
\Gamma=(x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n})
.
We can also write the conclusion as
of the form
P^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

where
\sigma
is the substitution mapping each
x_{i}
to
M_{i}
.
More precisely:

Definition 3.9 (Compatibility).

Let
\Gamma
be an environment and
\sigma
be a substitution.
We say that
\Gamma
is
compatible
with
\sigma
,
written
\Gamma\vDash\sigma
,
if for every
x:A\in\Gamma
we have that
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Lemma 3.10 (Universality).

Let
\Gamma\vDash\sigma

and
M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Then
M^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Proof.

We sketch the proof here; see
section

A.2
for details.
The idea is first to standardize
M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

using
proposition

3.7

to obtain
M^{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Let
n
be the length of this weak head reduction.
Write
|A|
for the size
6
6

6

Defined by

|\mathbf{a}|\,\mathrel{:=}\,1
and

|A\Rightarrow B|\,\mathrel{:=}\,1+|A|+|B|
.

of the type
A
,
and
|\Gamma|
for the maximum size of a type occurring in
\Gamma
,

i.e.

|\Gamma|\,\mathrel{:=}\,\max\{|A|\ |\ \exists x.\,(x:A\in\Gamma)\}
.
We show that
M^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
holds
by induction on the lexicographic measure
(|\Gamma|,n)
.
The interesting case is when
M
is of the form

M=\mathtt{W}[x\,N]
where
x^{\Gamma}
is an abstraction.
Then
x:(A\Rightarrow B)\in\Gamma

for certain types
A,B
, and the weak head reduction is of the form:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\Gamma}=\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}\,N^{\Gamma}]=\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[(\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,N^{\Gamma}]\\
&\to_{w}&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star\end{array}

The tail of the reduction sequence

\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

can be split into two reductions
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

and
\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
,
each shorter than the original one.

Consider the metaterm
\mathtt{W}[y]
,
where
y
is fresh, and

\Gamma_{1}:=\Gamma\cup\{y:B\}
,
and
\sigma_{1}:=\sigma[y\mapsto x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}]
.
The i.h. may be applied since
\Gamma_{1}\vDash\sigma_{1}

and
|\Gamma_{1}|=|\Gamma|

and
\mathtt{W}[y]^{\Gamma_{1}}=\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

is shorter than the original reduction.
So we obtain that
\mathtt{W}[y]^{\sigma_{1}}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
,
which means that

(I)
:
\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Recall that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
The i.h. may be applied a second time since
\Gamma\vDash\sigma

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
is shorter than
the original reduction.
So we obtain
(II)
:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Finally, consider the metaterm
\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]

where
z
is fresh, and
\Gamma_{2}:=\{z:A\}
,
and
\sigma_{2}:=[z\mapsto N^{\sigma}]
.
The i.h. may be applied a third time, since
\Gamma_{2}\vDash\sigma_{2}
holds by
(II)
,
and
|\Gamma_{1}|>|\Gamma|
,
and
\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

holds by
(I)
.
We obtain that

\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]^{\sigma_{2}}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
,
but

\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]^{\sigma_{2}}=\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,N^{\sigma}]=M^{\sigma}

so we are done.
∎

Finally, we can show:

Theorem 3.11 (Soundness).

If
\Gamma\vdash t:A
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:A
.
In the
variable
case, suppose that

\Gamma\vdash x:A
holds, so
x:A\in\Gamma
.
Then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

by correctness (
lemma

3.8
).

In the
abstraction
case,
we have that
t=\lambda x.\,s

and
A=(B\Rightarrow C)
,
and
\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,s:B\Rightarrow C

is derived from
\Gamma,x:B\vdash s:C
.
By
\alpha
-conversion, we may assume that
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.
Then we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\Rightarrow C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,s)^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,s^{\Gamma})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\{x:={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}\})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}s^{\Gamma,x:B}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\text{i.h. }}\star
.

Finally, in the
application
case,
we have that
t=s\,u

and
\Gamma\vdash s\,u:A

is derived from
\Gamma\vdash s:B\Rightarrow A

and
\Gamma\vdash u:B
.
By i.h. on the first premise,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\Rightarrow A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}s^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
which by definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\Rightarrow A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}

means that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
By i.h. on the second premise, we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}u^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Let
y
be a fresh variable,
consider the metaterm
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,y)
,
the typing context
\Delta:=\{y:B\}
,
and the substitution
\sigma:=\{y\mapsto u^{\Gamma}\}
.
Remark that

\Delta\vDash\sigma

so by universality (
lemma

3.10
)
we have that

({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,y))^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

implies

({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,y))^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Performing the substitutions, this means that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

implies

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,u^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
. The antecedent of the implication holds, so

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\Gamma}\,u^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
∎

3.2.
Completeness of
{\lambda}^{\to}
with respect to
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}

Completeness is the statement that
realizable types are provable
,

i.e.
that if
M

is a good metaterm such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

then
\vdash A
holds in minimal logic.
As we did for soundness, we have to generalize the statement in
order to account for possibly open terms.

The generalized statement of completeness says that if

M
is a good metaterm such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

then there exists a term
t
such that
\Gamma\vdash t:A
.
Moreover,
t
is such that
M\mathrel{=_{\beta}}t
.
In fact,
t
can be chosen to be the
\beta
-normal form of
M
.

Measuring the size of realizers.

The proof of completeness proceeds by induction on an integer

\lvert M\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}
called the
proof size
.
It is defined for any pure metaterm
M
,

even if it is untypable
, assuming that
M
has a
\beta
-normal form.
Recall the well-known fact that the following grammar of
normal terms

characterizes exactly the set of terms in
\beta
-normal form
(which coincides with the set of
pure metaterms
in normal form):

Definition 3.12 (Neutral and normal terms).

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\textsc{Neutral terms }&M^{\uparrow}&::=&\,\,x\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\hskip 14.22636pt(n\geq 0)\\
\textsc{Normal terms }&M^{\downarrow}&::=&\,\,M^{\uparrow}\,\,\mid\,\,\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow}\end{array}

Definition 3.13 (Proof size).

The
proof size
of a normal term
M^{\downarrow}
with respect to a environment
\Gamma

and a type
A

is a non-negative integer written
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}

and defined as follows, by induction on the shape of
M^{\downarrow}
,
according to
definition

3.12
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}ll}\lvert x\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}&\,\mathrel{:=}&\lx@intercol\!\!\!\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{{\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{i}}}\}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&\lx@intercol\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(if $x:(A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow A_{n}\Rightarrow A^{\prime})\in\Gamma$)}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\lvert x\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}&\,\mathrel{:=}&0&\text{(otherwise)}\\
\lvert\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}&\,\mathrel{:=}&\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:B\vdash C}&\text{(if $A=(B\Rightarrow C)$)}\\
\lvert\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}&\,\mathrel{:=}&0&\text{(otherwise)}\end{array}

The definition is generalized for any pure metaterm
M

as long as it has a normal form
M^{\downarrow}
,
defining
\lvert M\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}
.

One key observation is that

\lvert M\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\geq\lvert M\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.
Another key observation is that
if
x:(A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow A_{n}\Rightarrow A^{\prime})\in\Gamma

then
\lvert xM_{1}\ldots M_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A^{\prime}}>\lvert M_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{i}}

for every
1\leq i\leq n

(see
lemmas

A.9
and
A.10
in the
appendix for details).

Theorem 3.14 (Completeness).

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
M
is good, then its
\beta
-normal form
is a term
M^{\downarrow}
such that
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.

Proof.

We sketch the proof here; see
section

A.3
for details.
Let
M^{\downarrow}
be the
\beta
-normal form of
M
, which is a normal term.
We proceed by induction on
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}
.
Write
A=(A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a})
.
Note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be fresh variables,
and consider the metaterm
M\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}

and the environment
\Delta:=(\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n})
.
Then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n})^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Let
N^{\downarrow}
be the
\beta
-normal form of the application

M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}
.
By confluence (
proposition

3.6
),

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Note that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash\mathbf{a}}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash\mathbf{a}}
.
We consider two cases, depending on whether
N^{\downarrow}

is an abstraction or a neutral term.

If
N^{\downarrow}
is an
abstraction
,

i.e.

N^{\downarrow}=\lambda y.\,P
,
then we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\lambda y.\,P^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda y.\,P)^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
which is impossible,
because the reducts of an abstraction are always abstractions,
so in particular
\lambda y.\,P^{\Delta}

cannot have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
as a reduct.

If
N^{\downarrow}
is a
neutral term
,
then
N^{\downarrow}=x\,N_{1}\ldots N_{m}
,
and we know that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\Delta}\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Note that
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)

because otherwise we would have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\not{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}\star
, a contradiction.

Suppose then that
x:B\in\Delta
for some
B
.
Without loss of generality, write

B=(B_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow B_{k}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b})
.
We write
C^{?}P
to abbreviate
\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}P)

assuming that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(P)
.
Note that:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}lll}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\Delta}\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\,\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\,\,\star\end{array}

The cases
k<m
and
k>m
are impossible because the reduction
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})
would become “stuck”
(see
theorem

A.11
in the appendix for a detailed proof).
Hence
k=m

and we must have that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

for every
1\leq i\leq k
;
otherwise reduction would also become “stuck”.
Then we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{k}^{\Delta})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}\to\star
, and the only way this can happen is if
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}
.

For each
1\leq i\leq k

note that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash\mathbf{a}}>\lvert N_{i}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash B_{i}}

holds,
and note also that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
so by i.h.
\Delta\vdash N^{\downarrow}_{i}:B_{i}
.
Since
x:(B_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow B_{k}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a})\in\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
,
we can derive the judgment

\Delta\vdash x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}:\mathbf{a}
.
Take
t:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}
.
Note that:

\begin{array}[]{llll}t&=&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}\\
&\mathrel{=_{\beta}}&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N_{1}\ldots N_{k}\\
&=&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,N^{\downarrow}\\
&\mathrel{=_{\beta}}&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}&\text{as $M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}N^{\downarrow}$}\\
&\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}&M^{\downarrow}&\text{using $n$ steps of $\eta$-reduction}\end{array}

Thus
t\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}M
.
By confluence of
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
, we have

t\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}M
.
Moreover, since
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
and
\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}
commute and
M^{\downarrow}
is in
\beta
-normal form,
the situation is
t\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}M
.

Finally, since
\Delta=(\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n})
,
we have that

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}:A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}
,
that is

\Gamma\vdash t:A
.
Since
t\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}
,
by subject reduction we conclude
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.
∎

4.
Extension to Second-Order Logic

One way to extend the epistemic realizability interpretation
from the previous section
would be to define verifiers and generators for additional propositional
connectives.
For example, one might define the verifier for conjunction as

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\land B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\pi_{1}(M){?}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\pi_{2}(N)
,
and the generator as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\land B}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})
.
However, it is not immediately obvious how to define verifiers and generators
for
positive
connectives such as disjunction (
A\lor B
).

We take a different route, and extend the interpretation from minimal logic to
second-order logic
, incorporating second-order universal
quantification. It is well-known that other logical connectives
(including the intuitionistic propositional connectives),
as well as inductive datatypes (natural numbers, lists, trees, etc.)
can be expressed in second-order logic,
e.g.
using Böhm–Berarducci encodings
(
6
)
.

The main results in this section are
soundness
(
theorem

4.12
)
and
completeness
(
theorem

4.14
).
While the proof of completeness is an adaptation of the one for minimal logic,
the proof of soundness requires a fundamentally different approach.

The second-order
\lambda
-calculus (
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F}}
).

We begin by recalling the typed
\lambda
-calculus corresponding
to second-order logic, namely Girard–Reynolds System F
(
23
;
45
)
.
We assume given disjoint denumerable sets
of
type-variables
(
a,b,\ldots
),
and
eigenvariables
(
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\ldots
).
The sets of
types
and
terms

are given by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\textsc{Types }&A,B,\ldots&::=&a\mid\mathbf{a}\mid A\Rightarrow B\mid\forall a.\,A\\
\textsc{Terms }&t,s,\ldots&::=&x\mid\lambda x.\,t\mid t\,s\mid\Lambda{a}.\,t\mid t\,A\end{array}

The (second-order) universal quantifier
\forall a.\,A

binds the occurrences of
a
in
A
.
Eigenvariables are similar to type-variables, but they
are assumed to never occur bound in types or terms (although it will be possible that they occur bound in metaterms).
A type is
atomic
if it is either a type-variable or an eigenvariable.

From the propositions-as-types point of view,

\lambda x.\,t
and
t\,s

encode introduction and elimination of the implication,
while
\Lambda{a}.\,t
and
t\,A

encode introduction and elimination of the second-order universal quantifier.
Typing judgments are defined with the three typing rules for
{\lambda}^{\to}
given
in the previous section, plus the two following rules:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:A\hskip 14.22636pta\notin\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)}{\Gamma\vdash\Lambda{a}.\,t:\forall a.\,A}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:\forall a.\,A}{\Gamma\vdash t\,B:A\{a:=B\}}{\texttt{}}

We define
\beta
and
\eta
-reduction as the union of the following
rules, closed by arbitrary contexts:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\HS}r@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}(\lambda x.\,t)\,s&\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}&t\{x:=s\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(\Lambda{a}.\,t)\,A&\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}&t\{a:=A\}\\
\lambda x.\,t\,x&\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}&t\,\,\,\,\text{if $x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\Lambda{a}.\,t\,a&\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}&t\,\,\,\,\text{if $a\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}\end{array}

Both notions of reduction are defined over arbitrary terms,
even if they are not typable, but they can also be restricted to
typable terms, using the well-known property that they preserve
types.

The second-order metacalculus (
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
).

The set of metaterms of
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is given by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lrlllll}\lx@intercol\textsc{Metaterms}\hskip 14.22636ptM,N,\ldots::=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&x&\text{variable }&\mid&\star&\text{success }\\
&\mid&\lambda x.\,M&\text{term-abs. }&\mid&M{?}N&\text{guard }\\
&\mid&M\,N&\text{term-app. }&\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}&\text{generator}\\
&\mid&\Lambda{a}.\,M&\text{type-abs. }&\mid&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\text{verifier}\\
&\mid&M\,A&\text{type-app. }&\mid&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,M&\text{fresh eigenvariable }\end{array}

A metaterm is
pure
if it is built using only the five
productions on the left-hand side of the grammar.
Terms of
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F}}
are identified with pure metaterms of
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
A metaterm is
good
if it is pure and
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
-normalizing.

Syntactical differences between
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

The relationship between
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F}}

is analogous to that of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\lambda}^{\to}
.
We mention some of the differences between
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
First, besides the usual abstraction and application,

\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

also includes abstractions
\Lambda{a}.\,M

and applications
M\,A
,
which abstract over types.
Second,
the syntax of the metacalculus for minimal logic
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}

of the previous section
included only generators and verifiers of the forms

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
,
where
\mathbf{a}
was an atomic type.
Generators and verifiers for more complex
types were
defined
operators
(
definition

3.1
).
Instead, the second-order metacalculus
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

includes generators and verifiers for
arbitrary
types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
) directly on the syntax.
This is necessary because in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
type-variables may become
instantiated
dynamically
,
for example
(\Lambda{a}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{a})\,A\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
.
Third,

\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
includes a constructor
\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,M

that binds a local eigenvariable
\mathbf{a}
,
representing a
symbolic
or
constant
type,
in the scope of
M
.
This construct is used to verify universal quantifiers.

Reduction in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Weak head contexts
are given by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}\textsc{Weak head contexts}&\mathtt{W}&::=&\Box\mid\mathtt{W}\,M\mid\mathtt{W}\,A\mid\mathtt{W}{?}M\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\mathtt{W}\mid\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,\mathtt{W}\end{array}

The
reduction
relation in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, written
\to
,
is given by the union of the rewriting rules below,
closed by arbitrary contexts.
The binary relation of
weak head reduction
, written
\to_{w}
,
is also given by the union of the rewriting rules below,
but closed by weak head contexts.

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\text{\footnotesize{(1)}}&(\lambda x.\,M)\,N&\to&M\{x:=N\}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(2)}}&(\Lambda{a}.\,M)\,A&\to&M\{a:=A\}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(3)}}&\star{?}M&\to&M\\
\text{\footnotesize{(4)}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}&\to&\star\\
\text{\footnotesize{(5)}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}&\to&\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\\
\text{\footnotesize{(6)}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})\\
\text{\footnotesize{(7)}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\forall a.\,A}&\to&\Lambda{a}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(8)}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\forall a.\,A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\to&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,\mathbf{a})&\text{($\mathbf{a}$ fresh)}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(9)}}&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,M&\to&M&\text{(if $\mathbf{a}\notin\mathsf{fv}(M)$)}\end{array}

Rules 1 and 2 are
\beta
-reduction rules for term-abstraction and type-abstraction.
Rule 3 is the same as in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Rule 4 is the same as in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
but only applies when
\mathbf{a}
is an
eigenvariable
,
not a type-variable.
Rules 5 and 6 implement verification and generation of the implication,
with the same behavior as in minimal logic (
definition

3.1
)
but through reduction rules instead of definitional equations.
Rules 7 and 8 are the most interesting ones, as they
implement verification and generation of the second-order universal quantifier,
which is the new connective.
Rule 7 states that the generic realizer for
\forall a.\,A

is a type-abstraction that receives a type
a
and returns the
generic realizer
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
, where
A
may depend on
a
.
Rule 8 states that to verify whether
M
is a realizer for
A

one has to check whether the application
M\,\mathbf{a}

is a realizer for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
, where
\mathbf{a}
is
a fresh eigenvariable.
Eigenvariables represent “symbolic” or “constant” types.
Finally, rule 9 removes the fresh eigenvariable introduction
when it becomes unnecessary.

Example 4.1 (Encoding of conjunction).

Consider the usual second-order encoding of conjunction,

A\land B\,\mathrel{:=}\,\forall c.\,(A\Rightarrow B\Rightarrow c)\Rightarrow c
.
Then
\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\Lambda{c}.\,\lambda f.\,f\,x\,y

realizes
\land
-introduction:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow(\mathbf{a}\land\mathbf{b}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\Lambda{c}.\,\lambda f.\,f\,x\,y)\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\land\mathbf{b}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{c}.\,\lambda f.\,f\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\\
\to&\nu{\mathbf{c}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c})\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{c}.\,\lambda f.\,f\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,\mathbf{c})\\
\to&\nu{\mathbf{c}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c})\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda f.\,f\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\\
\to&\nu{\mathbf{c}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda f.\,f\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}})\\
\to&\nu{\mathbf{c}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&\nu{\mathbf{c}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{c}}\,\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\,\,\nu{\mathbf{c}}.\,\star\,\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\,\,\star\end{array}

and
\lambda p.\,p\,\mathbf{a}\,(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x)

realizes
\land
-elimination of the first conjunct:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\mathbf{a}\land\mathbf{b})\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda p.\,p\,\mathbf{a}\,(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x))\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\land\mathbf{b}}\,\mathbf{a}\,(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x))\\
\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{c}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow c)\Rightarrow c})\,\mathbf{a}\,(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x))\\
\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{(\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a})\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}}\,(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x))\\
\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda z.\,(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}z){?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}))\,(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x))\\
\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x)){?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}})\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\star{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}})\,\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\,\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,\,\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\,\,\star\end{array}

Rewriting properties of
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Unlike weak head reduction in the simply typed case,
weak head reduction is
not
deterministic in the second-order case.
For example,
if
M\to_{w}M^{\prime}

then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\to_{w}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\to_{w}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\prime}
.
However, it is easy to show that weak head reduction in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
enjoys
a strong form of confluence (see
section

A.1
for details):

Proposition 4.2.

Weak head reduction
\to_{w}
is subcommutative.

Regarding confluence of reduction, note that
the fourth rewriting rule of
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
not left-linear
.
However, because eigenvariables cannot be instantiated,
confluence can still be established following standard techniques:
defining a notion of
simultaneous reduction
(
\rhd
) such that

{\to}\subseteq{\rhd}\subseteq{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}

and such that
\rhd
satisfies the diamond property.
(A detailed proof is provided in
section

B.2
):

Proposition 4.3.

Reduction in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is confluent.

As in the case of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
reductions to
\star
in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

can be standardized to weak head reductions.
We omit the proof of standardization due to lack of space,
but see
section

B.3
for details.
The proof relies on an
intersection type system

(
11
)
,
in particular with
non-idempotent intersection

(
21
;
9
)
.
These type systems can be used to
characterize
dynamic properties
of programs such as head, weak, and strong normalization
(
8
)
,
and it is well known they can be used to prove standardization results
(
28
)
.

Proposition 4.4 (Standardization).

If
M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
then
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Proof.

See
section

B.3
for the proof.
The idea is to propose a type system
\mathcal{T}

based on
non-idempotent intersection types
,
with two key properties.
First,
weak head subject reduction
ensures that
if
M\to_{w}M^{\prime}

and
M
is typable in
\mathcal{T}

then
M^{\prime}
is typable in
\mathcal{T}
,
where moreover the
size
of the derivation tree for

M^{\prime}
is strictly smaller than the one for
M
.
Second,
subject expansion
ensures that
if
M\to M^{\prime}

and
M^{\prime}
is typable in
\mathcal{T}

then
M
is typable in
\mathcal{T}
.

Suppose that
M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
The constant
\star
is trivially typable in
\mathcal{T}
,
so by subject expansion
M
is also typable.
Let
M\to_{w}M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}\ldots
be a maximal sequence
of weak head reduction steps.
The sequence must be finite because weak head subject reduction ensures
that the size of the typing derivation decreases.
So there exists
n
such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}M_{n}

and
M_{n}
is a
\to_{w}
-normal form.
Since
M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}M_{n}
and
M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
by confluence (
proposition

4.3
) one concludes that

M_{n}=\star
.
∎

4.1.
Soundness of
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F}}
with respect to
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

We start by mentioning that the
correctness
lemma is easy to
prove for
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
by induction on the size of
A

(see
lemma

B.14
in the appendix for the proof):

Lemma 4.5 (Correctness).

If
A
has no free type-variables then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Note that correctness only holds for types
without free type-variables
.
For example,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{a}

is irreducible (since
a
is a type-variable),
while
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\to\star
holds
for an eigenvariable
\mathbf{a}
.

Unfortunately, it is not clear how to extend the syntactic
proof of soundness for minimal logic (
theorem

3.11
)
to second-order logic.
The kind of difficulties that arise are quite typical in
impredicative

settings.
In particular, the proof of
universality

for the metacalculus for minimal logic
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
(
lemma

3.10
)
proceeded by induction using a well-founded measure that depended on the size
of types.
This measure cannot be easily adapted to the second-order metacalculus

\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, because contracting a redex
(\forall a.\,M)\,A

results in a metaterm
M\{a:=A\}
in which, for example,
a generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}
occurring inside
M

becomes a generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\{a:=A\}}
for a potentially
larger type.

Verification candidates.

To prove soundness, we use a notion we call
verification candidates
,
inspired by reducibility candidates in Girard’s proof of strong normalization
for System F
(
23
)
.
A
verification candidate
is a set of metaterms enjoying certain
closure properties.
The proof technique essentially works by mapping each type
A
to a verification candidate
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
A first key lemma ensures that any metaterm
M\in\llbracket A\rrbracket

is accepted by the verifier for
A
,

i.e.
that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
A second key lemma ensures that each typable term of type
A

belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
As usual in these cases,
the statements of all results have to be generalized
in order to consider the interpretation
\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta

of a type
A
under an appropriate mapping
\eta
,
called a
candidate-assignment
,
that provides an interpretation for free type-variables.

Definition 4.6 (Verification candidates).

Let
A
be a type.
A set of metaterms
X
is an
A
-verification candidate

if the three following conditions hold:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\in X
,

(2)
if
M\in X
, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
,

(3)
if
M
,
M^{\prime}
are metaterms such that
M\to_{w}M^{\prime}
, then
M\in X
if and only if
M^{\prime}\in X
.

We write
\mathcal{VC}_{A}
for the set of all
A
-verification candidates.

Interpreting types as verification candidates.

A
type-substitution
is a function
\sigma
mapping each type-variable
a

to a type
\sigma(a)
.
A
candidate-assignment
is a function
\eta
mapping each type-variable
a

to a pair
(A,X)
, where
A
is a
closed
type,
i.e.
a type without free type-variables,
and
X
is an
A
-verification candidate.
We write
\eta[a\mapsto(A,X)]
for the candidate-assignment such that

(\eta[a\mapsto(A,X)])(a)=(A,X)

and

(\eta[a\mapsto(A,X)])(b)=\eta(b)

for any type-variable
b
other than
a
.
For each candidate-assignment
\eta
, we define its
first projection

as the type-substitution
\tilde{\eta}
such that

\tilde{\eta}(a)=A
whenever
\eta(a)=(A,X)
.
