By abuse of notation, sometimes we write
\eta
for the first projection of
the candidate-assignment
\eta
instead of
\tilde{\eta}

(
e.g.

A^{\eta}
stands for
A^{\tilde{\eta}}
).

Definition 4.7 (Candidate interpretation of types).

Let
A
be a type and
\eta
be a candidate-assignment.
We define a set of metaterms
\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta
by recursion on
A

as follows:

1.

\llbracket a\rrbracket\eta\,\mathrel{:=}\,X
if
\eta(a)=(A,X)
.

2.

\llbracket\mathbf{a}\rrbracket\eta

is the set of metaterms
M
such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
.

3.

\llbracket A\Rightarrow B\rrbracket\eta

is the set of metaterms
M
such that:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(A\Rightarrow B)^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
, and

for every
N\in\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta

we have that
M\,N\in\llbracket B\rrbracket\eta
.

4.

\llbracket\forall a.\,A\rrbracket\eta

is the set of metaterms
M

such that:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\forall a.\,A)^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
, and

for every closed type
B
and every
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{B}
,

we have that
M\,B\in\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B,X)]
.

Below, we mention three key properties of the interpretation of types.
We have omitted the detailed proofs but they can be found in

section

B.4
.
First, the interpretation of a type
A
always yields
an
A
-verification candidate.
More precisely, the following
adequacy
lemma holds
(see
lemma

B.15
for the proof):

Lemma 4.8 (Adequacy for types).

\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta\in\mathcal{VC}_{A^{\eta}}

Second, the two lemmas below are typical in reducibility interpretations
and related techniques, such as logical relations.
The first lemma expresses an
irrelevance
property,
and the second one expresses a
substitution
property.
(See
lemma

B.16
and
lemma

B.17

for the proofs):

Lemma 4.9.

If
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
agree on the free type-variables of
A
,
then
\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta_{1}=\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta_{2}
.

Lemma 4.10.

\llbracket A\{a:=B\}\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B\rrbracket\eta)]

Proving soundness through verification candidates.

Before stating and proving the main result, we require a few more definitions.
A
substitution
is a function
\gamma

mapping each variable to a metaterm.
The
generative substitution
for an environment
\Gamma

is written
\underline{\Gamma}

and defined as the substitution
such that
\underline{\Gamma}(x)={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
holds for every
x:A\in\Gamma
,
and
\underline{\Gamma}(x)=x
holds for every
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.
By abuse of notation, sometimes we write just
\Gamma
for the generative
substitution, rather than
\underline{\Gamma}
.
If
\eta
is a candidate-assignment,
\Gamma
is an environment,
and
\gamma
is a substitution,
we say that
\gamma
is
compatible
with
\Gamma
under
\eta
,
written
\Gamma\vDash^{\eta}\gamma
,
if for every variable
x:A\in\Gamma

we have that
x^{\gamma}\in\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta
.

The main lemma to prove soundness roughly states that
all terms of type
A
are elements of the
A
-verification candidate given by
\llbracket A\rrbracket
. The precise statement is more general,
to account for the presence of potentially free variables and
type-variables:

Lemma 4.11 (Adequacy for terms).

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:A
and
\Gamma\vDash^{\eta}\gamma
.
Then
t^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the typing judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:A
.
See
lemma

B.18
for the proof.
∎

Theorem 4.12 (Soundness).

Let
\Gamma,t,A
be such that they have no free type-variables
7
7

7

If they have free type-variables,
they can be substituted by fresh eigenvariables to apply the theorem.
For example the judgment
x:a\vdash x:a
holds
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{a}\not{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}}\star
,
but substituting
a
by a fresh eigenvariable
\mathbf{a}

we have that
x:\mathbf{a}\vdash x:\mathbf{a}

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

.
If
\Gamma\vdash t:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{a}
be a fixed eigenvariable,
and let
\llbracket\mathbf{a}\rrbracket
be the set of metaterms
M

such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
.
Observe that
\eta(a)=(\mathbf{a},\llbracket\mathbf{a}\rrbracket)
is a candidate-assignment.
Note that
\Gamma\vDash^{\eta}\underline{\Gamma}
,
where
\underline{\Gamma}
is the generative substitution for
\Gamma
,
because if
x:A\in\Gamma
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

by correctness (
lemma

4.5
).
Then by
lemma

4.11

we have
t^{\eta\Gamma}\in\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta
.
Finally, by
lemma

4.8

\llbracket A\rrbracket\eta
is a
A^{\eta}
-verification candidate,
so
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\eta\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Since
A
and
t
have no free type-variables this means that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
∎

4.2.
Completeness of
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F}}
with respect to
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

Fortunately, the technique to show completeness for minimal logic (
theorem

3.14
)
can be extended to the second-order case.
The details of the completeness proof can be found in
section

A.3
.
Here we only provide some of the key definitions,
and specifically the
proof size
(generalizing
definition

3.13
).

Characterization of normal forms.

An
input
(
I,J,\ldots
)
is either a metaterm or a type (given by the grammar
I::=M\mid A
).
If
\vec{I}=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})
is a sequence of inputs,
we write
M\,\vec{I}
or
M\,I_{1}\ldots I_{n}

for the application of
M
to the
n
inputs.
The sets of
pseudo-redexes
,
head forms
,
normal inputs
,

neutral terms
, and
normal terms

are given mutually inductively by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lr@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l}\textsc{Pseudo-redexes}&\Psi,\ldots&::=&(\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow})\,A\,\,\mid\,\,(\Lambda{a}.\,M^{\downarrow})\,N^{\downarrow}\\
\textsc{Head forms }&H,H^{\prime},\ldots&::=&x\mid\Psi\\
\textsc{Normal inputs }&I^{\downarrow},J^{\downarrow},\ldots&::=&M^{\downarrow}\,\,\mid\,\,A\\
\textsc{Neutral terms }&M^{\uparrow},N^{\uparrow},\ldots&::=&H\,I^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots I^{\downarrow}_{n}\\
\textsc{Normal terms }&M^{\downarrow},N^{\downarrow},\ldots&::=&M^{\uparrow}\,\,\mid\,\,\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow}\,\,\mid\,\,\Lambda{a}.\,M^{\downarrow}\end{array}

It is a routine exercise to show that pure metaterms in
\beta
-normal form correspond exactly to normal terms.

Input matching.

In the case of minimal logic, it is clear at a glance
that a term of type
A

can take as arguments a list of
n
terms
(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})

only if
A
is of the form
A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow A^{\prime}
.
In such case, we write

A\,/\,(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
We generalize this predicate to the second-order case,
writing
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

where
A,A^{\prime}
are types,
\vec{I}
is a list of inputs,
and
\vec{A}
a list of types. Inductively,
we declare that
A\,/\,\varnothing:\varnothing\Rrightarrow A
holds,
and:

\frac{B\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{B}\Rrightarrow B^{\prime}}{(A\Rightarrow B)\,/\,(M,\vec{I}):(A,\vec{B})\Rrightarrow B^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{A\{a:=B\}\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow C^{\prime}}{\forall a.\,A\,/\,(B,\vec{I}):(B,\vec{C})\Rrightarrow C^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}

For example,

(\forall a.\,(a\Rightarrow a)\Rightarrow a)\,/\,(B,M):(B,(B\Rightarrow B))\Rrightarrow B
.

Definition 4.13 (Proof size of normal terms).

The
proof size
of a normal term
M^{\downarrow}
with respect to a environment
\Gamma

and a type
A
is a non-negative integer written
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}

and defined as follows, by induction on the shape of the normal form
M^{\downarrow}
.
The definition is extended for normal inputs (
\lvert I^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}
)
by declaring that
\lvert I^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=0
when the input
I^{\downarrow}
is a type.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,\,\,}l}\lx@intercol\lvert x\,I^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots I^{\downarrow}_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{{\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B_{i}}}\}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\text{if $x:B\in\Gamma$ and $B\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{B}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}$}\\
&\text{$\vec{I}=(I^{\downarrow}_{1},\ldots,I^{\downarrow}_{n})$,
$\vec{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})$}\\
\lvert\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:B\vdash C}&\text{if $A=(B\Rightarrow C)$}\\
\lvert\Lambda{a}.\,M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B}&\text{if $A=(\forall a.\,B)$, assuming $a\notin\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)$}\\
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\,\mathrel{:=}\,0&\text{in any other case}\end{array}

Note in particular that
\lvert H\,I^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots I^{\downarrow}_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=0

if
H
is a pseudo-redex rather than a variable.

Theorem 4.14 (Completeness).

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
M
is good,
then its
\beta
-normal form is a term
M^{\downarrow}
such
that
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.

Proof.

The details of the proof may be found in
theorem

B.33
.
The proof proceeds by induction on the lexicographic pair

(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A},\lvert A\rvert)
, where
\lvert A\rvert
denotes
the size of the type
A
.
∎

5.
Extension to Higher-Order Logic

In this section, we discuss how to extend the epistemic realizability
interpretations of the previous sections to higher-order logic.
Recall that higher-order logic generalizes systems such as first and
second-order logic by allowing universal quantifiers to bind variables
of arbitrarily high order. Type-variables may represent not
only elements of the universe of discourse, but also predicates on
these elements, predicates on these predicates, and so on.
Due to its expressivity, it has been used as the basis of
functional programming languages such as
Haskell
,
and proof assistants such as
HOL Light
or
Isabelle/HOL
.
Many formalizations of higher-order logic can be found in the literature,
starting with Church’s simple theory of types
(
10
)
.
Our work is based on Girard’s System F
\omega

(
23
)
,
one of the vertices of Barendregt’s
\lambda
-cube
(
5
)
.
System F
\omega
is a
two-layered
typed
\lambda
-calculus with
three sorts of expressions:
kinds
,
types
, and
terms
.

The main results in this section are again

soundness
(
theorem

5.2
)
and
completeness
(
theorem

5.3
).

The higher-order
\lambda
-calculus (
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F\omega}}
).

We recall the typed
\lambda
-calculus corresponding
to higher-order logic, namely Girard’s System F
\omega

(
23
;
45
)
.
The set of
kinds
is given by the following grammar, including
base kinds (
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime},\ldots
) and a constant
\mathtt{Prop}
:

\textsc{Kinds }\hskip 14.22636ptK,J,\ldots::=\kappa\mid\mathtt{Prop}\mid K\to J

As in the second-order case,
we assume given disjoint sets of
type-variables
(
a,b,\ldots
)
and
eigenvariables
(
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\ldots
).
The set of
types
is given by the grammar:

\textsc{Types }\hskip 14.22636ptA,B,\ldots::=a\mid\mathbf{a}\mid\lambda{a^{K}}.\,A\mid A\,B\mid A\Rightarrow B\mid\forall{a^{K}}.\,A

A
kind-environment
(
\xi,\xi^{\prime},\ldots
)
is a finite set of assignments

a_{1}:K_{1},\ldots,a_{n}:K_{n},\mathbf{a}_{1}:K_{n+1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{m}:K_{n+m}
,
mapping type-variables and eigenvariables to kinds.
Kinding judgments are of the form
\xi\vdash A:K
,
meaning that the type
A
is well-kinded of kind
K
under the kind-environment
\xi
.
The typing rules are the expected ones.
In particular,

\xi\vdash A\Rightarrow B:\mathtt{Prop}

holds if
\xi\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}
and
\xi\vdash B:\mathtt{Prop}
hold;
and
\xi\vdash\forall{a^{K}}.\,A:\mathtt{Prop}

holds if
\xi,a:K\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}
holds.
We define
\beta
-convertibility
as the least congruence
generated by the usual
\beta
-reduction rule:

(\lambda{a^{K}}.\,A)\,B\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}A\{a:=B\}

Types of kind
\mathtt{Prop}
are called
propositions
.
For example, the type
\forall{P^{\kappa\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,\forall{f^{\kappa\to\kappa}}.\,\forall{a^{\kappa}}.\,(P\,a\Rightarrow P\,(f\,a))

is a proposition.
Note that type-variables bound by universal quantifiers may
play the role of predicate symbols (
e.g.

P
)
or function symbols (
e.g.

f
).

Terms and typing judgments.

Given a denumerable set of
variables
(
x,y,\ldots
),
the set of
terms
is given by the grammar:

\textsc{Terms }\hskip 14.22636ptt,s,\ldots::=x\mid\lambda x.\,t\mid t\,s\mid\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,t\mid t\,A

Note that the grammar of terms is similar to the one for
the second-order case, with the difference that type-variables bound by type-abstractions are decorated with their kind.
We write
\lambda x.\,t
instead of
\lambda x.\,t

to avoid confusion with the abstraction at the level of types.

An
environment
(
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\ldots
) is a finite set of

\{x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\}
that maps distinct
variables to types.
We introduce two further forms of judgment:

(1)

\xi\vdash\Gamma\,\texttt{env}
, meaning that the environment
\Gamma
is well-formed under the kind-environment
\xi

(
i.e.
it maps variables to
propositions
),
and
(2)

\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A
,
meaning that the term
t
is a proof of the proposition
A

under the environments
\xi
and
\Gamma
.
Valid judgments are given by:

\frac{(\xi\vdash A_{i}:\mathtt{Prop})_{i=1}^{n}}{\xi\vdash(x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n})\,\texttt{env}}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\xi\vdash(\Gamma,x:A)\,\texttt{env}}{\xi;\Gamma,x:A\vdash x:A}{\texttt{}}

\frac{\xi;\Gamma,x:A\vdash t:B}{\xi;\Gamma\vdash\lambda x^{A}.\,t:A\Rightarrow B}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A\Rightarrow B\hskip 14.22636pt\xi;\Gamma\vdash s:A}{\xi;\Gamma\vdash t\,s:B}{\texttt{}}

\frac{\xi,a:K;\Gamma\vdash t:A\hskip 14.22636pta\notin\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)}{\xi;\Gamma\vdash\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,t:\forall{a^{K}}.\,A}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:\forall{a^{K}}.\,A\hskip 14.22636pt\xi\vdash B:K}{\xi;\Gamma\vdash t\,B:A\{a:=B\}}{\texttt{}}

\frac{\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A\hskip 14.22636pt\xi\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}\hskip 14.22636pt\xi\vdash B:\mathtt{Prop}\hskip 14.22636ptA\mathrel{=_{\beta}}B}{\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:B}{\texttt{}}

The last rule is called the
conversion
rule.
We define
\beta
and
\eta
-reduction as the union of the following
rules, closed by arbitrary contexts:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\HS}r@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}(\lambda x.\,t)\,s&\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}&t\{x:=s\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,t)\,A&\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}&t\{a:=A\}\\
\lambda x.\,t\,x&\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}&t\,\,\,\,\text{if $x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,t\,a&\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}&t\,\,\,\,\text{if $a\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}\end{array}

The higher-order metacalculus (
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
)

Terms of the
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
calculus (called “metaterms”) are defined
as
equivalence classes of pre-metaterms
modulo a suitable equivalence relation.
We start by presenting the syntax of pre-metaterms of
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{lrlllll}\lx@intercol\textsc{Pre-metaterms }\hskip 14.22636ptM,N,\ldots::=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&x&\text{variable }&\mid&\star&\text{success }\\
&\mid&\lambda x.\,M&\text{term-abs. }&\mid&M{?}N&\text{guard }\\
&\mid&M\,N&\text{term-app. }&\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}&\text{generator}\\
&\mid&\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M&\text{type-abs. }&\mid&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\text{verifier}\\
&\mid&M\,A&\text{type-app. }&\mid&\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,M&\text{fresh eigenvariable }\end{array}

The notions of
pure
and
good
metaterms are defined as
for
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

The higher-order metacalculus
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}

is similar to second-order metacalculus
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
except for the fact that
\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M

and
\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,M
include a kind decoration.
This was not necessary in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

because all type-variables were morally always of kind
\mathtt{Prop}
.
The syntax of
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
includes generators
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}

and verifiers
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
for arbitrary types
A
,
which shall be restricted to be
propositions
.

Global kind-assignment.

Kinds and types of expressions in
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
depend
on a kind-environment
\xi
.
To alleviate the notation, we stick to the following well-established
convention
(
23
;
20
)
.
We assume that there is a global
kind-assignment

\Xi
,

i.e.
a function mapping each type-variable
a

and each eigenvariable
\mathbf{a}
to its kind.
We assume that the global kind-assignment has “enough”
variables of each kind,

i.e.
for each kind
K
there are denumerably infinite sets of type-variables and eigenvariables of kind
K
.
Whenever we have an expression like
\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M

that binds a type-variable of kind
K
,
and in case we need to reason about the body
M
,
we assume (by
\alpha
-renaming if necessary)
that
a
has been chosen in such a way that
\Xi(a)=K
,
and similarly for eigenvariables.
We write
\xi\subseteq\Xi

if the kind-environment
\xi
is
compatible

with
\Xi
,

i.e.
it assigns the same kinds as
\Xi
to type-variables and eigenvariables.
We write
\Xi\vdash A:K
to mean
that
\xi\vdash A:K
holds for some
\xi\subseteq\Xi
.
Similarly, we may write
\Xi\vdash\Gamma\,\texttt{env}

to mean that
\xi\vdash\Gamma\,\texttt{env}
holds,
and
\Xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A

to mean that
\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A
holds,
for some
\xi\subseteq\Xi

Well-kindedness for pre-metaterms.

The
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
calculi of previous sections
were completely untyped.
In contrast, in
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
we shall impose an invariant enforcing that
pre-metaterms be
well-kinded
.
Specifically, well-kindedness imposes the restriction that,
in all occurrences of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
the type
A
is a proposition,
and that in each type-application
M\,A

the type
A
is well-kinded.
For example,
\nu{\mathbf{a}^{\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
is well-kinded,
while
\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}

(in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
generates a “realizer” for a type that
is not a proposition) is
not
well-kinded.
The metacalculus is still untyped, in that metaterms are not required to be
well-typed; for example,
x\,x
is well-kinded.

Formally, we say that a pre-metaterm
M
is
well-kinded

if the predicate
\mathtt{wk}(M)
holds,
according to the following inductive rules:

\frac{}{\mathtt{wk}(x)}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{\mathtt{wk}(M)}{\mathtt{wk}(\lambda x.\,M)}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{\mathtt{wk}(M)\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{wk}(N)}{\mathtt{wk}(M\,N)}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{\mathtt{wk}(M)\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi(a)=K}{\mathtt{wk}(\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M)}{\texttt{}}

\frac{\mathtt{wk}(M)\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi\vdash A:K}{\mathtt{wk}(M\,A)}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{}{\mathtt{wk}(\star)}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{\mathtt{wk}(M)\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{wk}(N)}{\mathtt{wk}(M{?}N)}{\texttt{}}

\frac{\Xi\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}}{\mathtt{wk}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{\Xi\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{wk}(M)}{\mathtt{wk}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M)}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{\mathtt{wk}(M)\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi(\mathbf{a})=K}{\mathtt{wk}(\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,M)}{\texttt{}}

Note that the pre-metaterm
\Lambda{b^{\kappa\to\kappa}}.\,(\Lambda{a^{\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{a})\,b

is well-kinded but naive
\beta
-conversion
would yield
\Lambda{b^{\kappa\to\kappa}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{b}
,
which is not well-kinded.
Reduction in
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
shall be restricted to
ensure that well-kindedness is preserved.

Metaterms and
\tau
-equivalence.

The set of
metaterms
is obtained by quotienting the set of well-kinded pre-metaterms by the equivalence relation that identifies them if they are equal up to

\beta
-conversion of the types occurring in them.
Formally,
\tau
-equivalence

is written
M\equiv M^{\prime}
and obtained by the following inductive
definition, which assumes that
M
and
M^{\prime}
are well-kinded:

\frac{M\equiv M^{\prime}}{\lambda x.\,M\equiv\lambda x.\,M^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{M\equiv M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptN\equiv N^{\prime}}{M\,N\equiv M^{\prime}\,N^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}\,\,\,\,\frac{M\equiv M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi(a)=K}{\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M\equiv\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}

\frac{}{x\equiv x}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\equiv M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptA\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A^{\prime}}{M\,A\equiv M^{\prime}\,A^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{\star\equiv\star}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\equiv M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptN\equiv N^{\prime}}{M{?}N\equiv M^{\prime}{?}N^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}

\frac{A\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A^{\prime}}}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{A\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptM\equiv M^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\equiv{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\equiv M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi(\mathbf{a})=K}{\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,M\equiv\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,M^{\prime}}{\texttt{}}

We work with metaterms by writing pre-metaterms,
treating them
implicitly
modulo
\tau
-equivalence.
As usual in other settings (
e.g.
higher-order rewriting systems),
we say that a type-variable or eigenvariable occurs

free
in a metaterm
M
if it occurs free in
every
representative.
From now on, expressions
M,N,\ldots
denote metaterms rather
than pre-metaterms.
We shall not explicitly state lemmas showing that constructions can
be lifted from pre-metaterms to metaterms, but these lifting properties
are easy due to the standard fact that well-kinded types have
unique
\beta
-normal forms.

Reduction in
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

The set of
weak head contexts
is given by:

\mathtt{W}::=\Box\mid\mathtt{W}\,M\mid\mathtt{W}\,A\mid\mathtt{W}{?}M\mid{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\mathtt{W}\mid\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,\mathtt{W}

The
reduction
relation, written
\to
,
is given by the union of the reduction rules below,
closed by congruence under arbitrary contexts.
The
weak head reduction
relation, written
\to_{w}
,
is given by the union of the reduction rules below,
closed by congruence under weak head contexts.

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\text{\footnotesize{(1)}}&(\lambda x.\,M)\,N&\to&M\{x:=N\}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(2)}}&(\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,M)\,A&\to&M\{a:=A\}&\text{(if $\Xi\vdash A:K$)}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(3)}}&\star{?}M&\to&M\\
\text{\footnotesize{(4)}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}\vec{A}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}\vec{A}}&\to&\star\\
\text{\footnotesize{(5)}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\to&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})\\
\text{\footnotesize{(6)}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\forall{a^{K}}.\,A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M&\to&\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,\mathbf{a})&\text{($\mathbf{a}$ fresh)}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(7)}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}&\to&\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\\
\text{\footnotesize{(8)}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\forall{a^{K}}.\,A}&\to&\Lambda{a^{K}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\\
\text{\footnotesize{(9)}}&\nu{\mathbf{a}^{K}}.\,M&\to&M&\text{(if $\mathbf{a}\notin\mathsf{fv}(M)$)}\end{array}

The rewriting rules are almost exactly the same as to those of the
second-order metacalculus
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
There are two noteworthy differences.
First, rule 2 requires that the redex is
well-kinded
to proceed,

i.e.
that the actual argument
A

has the same kind as the formal parameter
a
.
For example, the step

(\Lambda{a^{\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{a\Rightarrow a})\,\mathbf{b}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b}}

is allowed if and only if
\mathbf{b}:\mathtt{Prop}\in\Xi
.
This side condition allows to show that
reduction
preserves well-kindedness
.

Second, note that if
A
is a well-kinded proposition without
free type-variables,
it must be exactly of one of three possible forms up to
\beta
-convertibility:
an applied eigenvariable
(\mathbf{a}\,A_{1}\ldots A_{n})
,
an implication
(B\Rightarrow C)
,
or a universal quantification
(\forall{a^{K}}.\,B)
.
The reduction rule 4 treats the case for
\mathbf{a}\,A_{1}\ldots A_{n}
,
written
\mathbf{a}\vec{A}
in the rule.
Contrast this with the rule for
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
which was just
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\to\star
.
In the second-order case, all eigenvariables were morally
propositions (of kind
\mathtt{Prop}
), and could not be applied,
while in
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F\omega}}
they can also play the role of
predicate symbols

(of kind
K_{1}\to\ldots\to K_{n}\to\mathtt{Prop}
).

Example 5.1 (Symmetry of Leibniz equality).

Let
A,B
be types of kind
K
,
and let
Leibniz equality
be defined by its
usual encoding

(A=_{K}B):=\forall{P^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,(P\,A\Rightarrow P\,B)
.
Let
Q_{P}:=\lambda{c^{K}}.\,(P\,c\Rightarrow P\,A)
.
Then the following reduction shows that
symmetry

is realizable:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}l}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(A=_{K}B)\Rightarrow(B=_{K}A){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda e.\,\Lambda{P^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,\lambda x.\,e\,Q_{P}\,(\lambda y.\,y)\,x)\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B=_{K}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{P^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A=_{K}B}\,Q_{P}\,(\lambda y.\,y)\,x)\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&\nu{\mathbf{p}^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{p}B\Rightarrow\mathbf{p}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A=_{K}B}\,Q_{\mathbf{p}}\,(\lambda y.\,y)\,x)\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&\nu{\mathbf{p}^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{p}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A=_{K}B}\,Q_{\mathbf{p}}\,(\lambda y.\,y)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{(\mathbf{p}B)})\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&\nu{\mathbf{p}^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{p}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{(Q_{\mathbf{p}}A\Rightarrow Q_{\mathbf{p}}B)}\,(\lambda y.\,y)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{p}B})\\
\equiv&\nu{\mathbf{p}^{K\to\mathtt{Prop}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{p}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{(\mathbf{p}A\Rightarrow\mathbf{p}A)\Rightarrow\mathbf{p}B\Rightarrow\mathbf{p}A}\,(\lambda y.\,y)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{p}B})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star\end{array}

Soundness and completeness of
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F\omega}}
with respect to
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

The rest of the development follows closely what we have done in
the second-order case (
section

4
).
In particular, the notion of
generative substitution

\underline{\Gamma}
is defined as before,
and
M^{\Gamma}
denotes
M^{\underline{\Gamma}}
.
The proofs of soundness and completeness
are extensions of those for the second-order case
(
theorem

4.12
and
theorem

4.14
).
The proof of completeness is a straightforward extension,
while the proof of soundness requires to parameterize the interpretation
with respect to an
indexed family of verification candidates
,
using types as indices, to be able to interpret higher-kinded types.
This follows well-established techniques
(
23
;
20
)
.
Details for the proof of soundness can be found in
section

C.1
.

Theorem 5.2 (Soundness).

Let
\Gamma,t,A
be such that they have no free type-variables.
If
\Xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Theorem 5.3 (Completeness).

Let
\Xi\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}

and
\mathtt{wk}(M)

and
\Xi\vdash\Gamma\,\texttt{env}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
M
is good,
then its
\beta
-normal form is a term
M^{\downarrow}
such
that
\Xi;\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.

6.
Conclusion

This paper formulates epistemic realizability interpretations
for
minimal
,
second
, and
higher-order logic
,
showing that each of the three systems is
sound

(
theorems

3.11
,
4.12
and
5.2
)
and
complete

(
theorems

3.14
,
4.14
and
5.3
).

Related work.

Our main motivation is philosophical: to explain the meaning of propositions
from a verificationist perspective, but imposing the condition that
the semantics is “epistemic”,
i.e.
that recognizing evidence for a
proposition is semi-decidable.
Similar concerns underly several foundational programs, including
Kreisel’s theory of constructions
(
30
)
,
Prawitz’s proof-theoretic semantics
(
43
)
,
Dummett’s anti-realist theory of meaning
(
15
)
,
and Martin-Löf’s meaning explanations
(
33
)
.
In those works, the meaning of a proposition is defined
by the conditions for making a judgment that asserts it,
while in our case the meaning of a proposition is defined
compositionally
,
without referring to the notion of judgment.
For example, the meaning of
A\Rightarrow B
is a pair of
programs
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B})

which are defined in terms of the meanings of
A
and
B
,

i.e.
in terms of the pairs of programs

({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})
and
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})
,
without making reference to a judgment like
A\vdash B
.

Our motivation is perhaps closest to that of
Girard’s Ludics
(
24
;
17
;
16
;
25
;
51
)
, in which the meaning of
propositions is given by the interaction between proofs and

counter-proofs
(related to
verifiers
)
which may include
daimons
(related to
generators
).

The notions of
correctness
and
universality
in this paper
resemble the notion of
harmony
of introduction and elimination
rules
(
15
;
55
)
, and specifically the conditions
of
local soundness
and
local completeness

(
13
;
42
)
.

The motivation behind realizability interpretations is to extract

computational content
from proofs.
Although some realizability interpretations extract
\lambda
-terms from proofs
(
e.g.
Kreisel’s),
as far as we know,
none of these intepretations express the verification procedure as a program.
An active field of research is that of Krivine realizability
(
31
)
,
as a means to extract computational content from
classical and
set theoretical reasoning principles
(
34
;
26
;
35
;
14
;
18
)
.

Limitations.

Our completeness theorems require that the realizer is “good”:
both
pure
and
\beta
-normalizing
. We conjecture that
they can be strengthened to avoid relying on
\beta
-normalization.
One strategy could be
to show that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

implies that
M
is
\beta
-normalizing,
through a non-idempotent intersection type
system (
cf.

proposition

4.4
).

It is unclear how to extend the semantics to more expressive
systems,
e.g.
allowing dependent elimination on datatypes.
Inductive and coinductive datatypes can be
encoded in type theories with
impredicative

universes
(
1
;
7
)
. While these encodings can be
reproduced in higher-order logic, the resulting datatypes
only support
non-dependent
elimination.

Potential application: “typeless types”.

One interesting aspect is that verifiers and
generators can be implemented directly
by the user

in an untyped programming language
that supports features such as first-class functions.
For example, the operators of
section

4

can be easily implemented in a language such as
Python
or a
Lisp
dialect.
The verifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}
can then be used to
dynamically

check whether an
untyped
program
M
behaves as a term
of type
A
, without inspecting the source code
8
8

8

This assumes, of course, that
M
has no side-effects.

.
This could be a way to integrate static and dynamic type
systems, as in gradual typing
(
48
)
.

In a similar spirit, epistemic realizability could potentially become
the basis of proof assistants in which proof checking is implemented
not by statically analyzing the proofs, but by dynamically running the
verifier. These tools would not require type annotations on the
source proofs, and they would not need to perform complex operations such
as elaboration or higher-order unification
9
9

9

Error reporting, however,
could be challenging.
.

Potential as a foundation for ultrafinitism.

Ultrafinitism is a branch of constructivism concerned with

feasibility

(
40
;
53
;
36
;
46
)
.
An ultrafinitist may deny that an expression like
2^{1000}
denotes a natural
number, on the grounds that computational resources are
bounded: a computation exceeding our available time is effectively
indistinguishable from a non-terminating one.
The question of providing logical foundations for ultrafinitism has
not been definitely settled,
as attested by some recent and ongoing works
(
19
;
32
;
27
)
.

The epistemic realizability semantics proposed in this paper could perhaps
provide the underlying semantics for an ultrafinitistic logic.
For example, taking the usual second-order encoding of natural numbers,

\mathbb{N}:=\forall a.\,(a\Rightarrow(a\Rightarrow a)\Rightarrow a)

and the usual definition of exponentiation in this setting,
it is feasible to check that the function
\lambda n.\,2^{n}

is a realizer for
\mathbb{N}\Rightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
as it amounts to a “short” calculation

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}\Rightarrow\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda n.\,2^{n})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(2^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbb{N}}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
On the other hand, it is unfeasible to check that
(\lambda n.\,2^{n})\,1000

is a realizer for
\mathbb{N}
,
as it would amount to a calculation

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda n.\,2^{n})\,1000)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}2^{1000}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
that exceeds our computational resources
10
10

10

Note that our soundness and completeness results entail that
the typing rule for application is valid, so from

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}\Rightarrow\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda n.\,2^{n})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}1000\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

one may conclude that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbb{N}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda n.\,2^{n})\,1000)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
A radical ultrafinitist would likely reject
the validity of the meta-theoretical reasoning used to justify this.

.
This seems consistent with the positions of some ultrafinitists,
including Sazonov’s proposal that only normal (cut-free) proofs should
be allowed
(
46
)
,
and Zeilberger’s view that general theorems about “all” integers
are actually statements about
symbolic
integers
(
54
)
.

Future work.

It would be interesting to provide a semantics for
positive connectives directly, without resorting to their second-order encodings.
For example, the generator
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\lor B}

should behave non-deterministically as either

\texttt{inj}_{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})
or
\texttt{inj}_{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})
.

Epistemic realizability relates typability (
\vdash t:A
)
and successful termination (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
).
This may provide a new strategy to connect simple types
and intersection types, which is the topic of recent work
(
41
)
.

As part of ongoing work, we attempt to extend the semantics
to classical propositional logic,
using a variant of Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
-calculus
(
39
)
.
We expect that in linear and classical calculi based on sequent calculus,
such as
\bar{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}

(
12
)
,
the duality between verifiers and generators should become more apparent,
with the verifier for
A
corresponding to the generator for
\neg A
.

An alternative way to provide a
epistemic
semantics for minimal logic is through an idea, due to Scott
(
47
)
, that each proposition
A

can be interpreted as an
idempotent operator

\llbracket A\rrbracket

and each typable term
\vdash t:A

as a
fixed point

\llbracket t\rrbracket
of the operator
\llbracket A\rrbracket
.
The problem is whether this idea can be extended to second and higher-order logic.

Appendix A
Minimal Logic — Properties of
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}

A.1.
Minimal Logic: Determinism of Weak Head Reduction (
proposition

3.5
)

To show that weak head reduction in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is deterministic,

i.e.
that if
N\to_{w}N_{1}
and
N\to_{w}N_{2}

then
N_{1}=N_{2}
,
it suffices to prove the following lemma:

Lemma A.1 (Uniqueness of the weak head redex).

Suppose that
\mathtt{W}_{1}[M_{1}]=\mathtt{W}_{2}[M_{2}]

where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are redexes.
Then
\mathtt{W}_{1}=\mathtt{W}_{2}
and
M_{1}=M_{2}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\mathtt{W}_{1}
:

1.

\mathtt{W}_{1}=\Box
: then
M_{1}=\mathtt{W}_{2}[M_{2}]
.
We claim that
\mathtt{W}_{2}=\Box
.
Indeed, if
\mathtt{W}_{2}\neq\Box

then
\mathtt{W}_{2}[M_{2}]
does not contain a redex at the root,
as can be seen by case analysis on the shape of the weak head
context
\mathtt{W}_{2}
and the redex
M_{2}
.
Hence
\mathtt{W}_{1}=\mathtt{W}_{2}=\Box
and
M_{1}=M_{2}
.

2.

\mathtt{W}_{1}=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{1}\,N
: note that
\mathtt{W}_{2}
cannot be empty,
because this would imply that
\mathtt{W}_{1}
is empty,
using a symmetrical argument as in the base case.
Hence
\mathtt{W}_{1}
and
\mathtt{W}_{2}
are both non-empty.
Since
\mathtt{W}_{1}[M_{1}]=\mathtt{W}_{2}[M_{2}]
by hypothesis,
then
\mathtt{W}_{2}
must be of the form
\mathtt{W}_{2}=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{2}\,N

and
\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{1}[M_{1}]=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{2}[M_{2}]
.
By i.h.,
\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{2}
and
M_{1}=M_{2}
.
To conclude, note that

\mathtt{W}_{1}=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{1}\,N=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{2}\,N=\mathtt{W}_{2}
.

3.

\mathtt{W}_{1}=\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{1}{?}N
:
similar to the previous case.

4.

\mathtt{W}_{1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\mathtt{W}^{\prime}_{1}
:
similar to the previous case.

∎

A.2.
Minimal Logic: Universality (
lemma

3.10
)

To prove universality, we start by showing some auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma A.2 (Splitting).

The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathtt{W}[M{?}N]\to_{w}^{n}\star

2.

There exist
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 0
such that

M\to_{w}^{n_{1}}\star

and
\mathtt{W}[N]\to_{w}^{n_{2}}\star

and
n=1+n_{1}+n_{2}
.

Proof.

(
1\implies 2
)
Suppose that
\mathtt{W}[M{?}N]\to_{w}^{n}\star
.
We proceed by induction on
n
.
The reduction cannot be empty, so
n>0
,
i.e.

n=1+m
for some
m\geq 0
,
and the reduction must be of the form

\mathtt{W}[M{?}N]\to_{w}P\to_{w}^{m}\star
.
We consider two subcases, depending on whether
the redex contracted in the first step is
M{?}N

or internal to
M
:

1.

If the contracted redex is
M{?}N
,
then
M=\star
,
and the reduction is of the form

\mathtt{W}[\star{?}N]\to_{w}\mathtt{W}[N]\to_{w}^{m}\star
.
Hence
M=\star\to_{w}^{0}\star

and
\mathtt{W}[N]\to_{w}^{m}\star
,
and taking
n_{1}:=0
and
n_{2}:=m
we have that

n=1+m=1+n_{1}+n_{2}
, as required.

2.

If the contracted redex is internal to
M
,
then the reduction is of the form

\mathtt{W}[M{?}N]\to_{w}\mathtt{W}[M^{\prime}{?}N]\to_{w}^{m}\star

where
M\to_{w}M^{\prime}
.
By i.h. there exist
m_{1},m_{2}

such that
M^{\prime}\to_{w}^{m_{1}}\star

and
\mathtt{W}[N]\to_{w}^{m_{2}}\star

and
m=1+m_{1}+m_{2}
.
Taking
n_{1}:=1+m_{1}
and
n_{2}:=m_{2}
,
we have that

M\to_{w}M^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{m_{1}}\star

where
n=1+m=2+m_{1}+m_{2}=1+n_{1}+n_{2}
.

(
2\implies 1
)
Suppose that
M\to_{w}^{n_{1}}\star
and
\mathtt{W}[N]\to_{w}^{n_{2}}\star
.
Then we have that

\mathtt{W}[M{?}N]\to_{w}^{n_{1}}\mathtt{W}[\star{?}N]\to_{w}\mathtt{W}[N]\to_{w}^{n_{2}}\star

of length
n=1+n_{1}+n_{2}
.
∎

Recall that a
redex
is a reducible expression.
In this specific setting, a metaterm
M
is a redex
if it matches the left-hand side of one of the rewriting
rules in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then:

Lemma A.3 (Substitution commutes with redexes).

If
R\to S
is a reduction step at the root
in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
then
R^{\sigma}\to S^{\sigma}

is a reduction step at the root in
\lambda^{\to}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward, by case analysis on the possible shapes of the redex
R
.
∎

Lemma A.4 (Substitution commutes with weak head contexts).

1.

If
\mathtt{W}
is a weak head context,
then
\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}
is a weak head context.

2.

\mathtt{W}[M]^{\sigma}=\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[M^{\sigma}]

Proof.

Both items are straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{W}
.
The key remark is that the hole of a weak head context does not lie
inside an abstraction, so in the expression

\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[M^{\sigma}]
there is no
danger of variable capture.
∎

Lemma A.5 (Origin of weak head redexes).

If
M^{\Gamma}
is of the form
\mathtt{W}[R]
,
where
R
is a redex, then exactly one of the following holds:

1.

There exist
\mathtt{W}_{0},R_{0}
such that

M=\mathtt{W}_{0}[R_{0}]

where
\mathtt{W}_{0}^{\Gamma}=\mathtt{W}

and
R_{0}^{\Gamma}=R

and
R_{0}
is a redex.

2.

There exist
\mathtt{W}_{0},x,N,y,P
such that

M=\mathtt{W}_{0}[x\,N]

where
\mathtt{W}_{0}^{\Gamma}=\mathtt{W}

and
x^{\Gamma}=\lambda y.\,P
.

3.

There exist
\mathtt{W}_{0},x,N
such that

M=\mathtt{W}_{0}[x{?}N]

where
\mathtt{W}_{0}^{\Gamma}=\mathtt{W}

and
x^{\Gamma}=\star
.

4.

There exist
\mathtt{W}_{0},x
such that

M=\mathtt{W}_{0}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x]

where
\mathtt{W}_{0}^{\Gamma}=\mathtt{W}

and
x^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
M
.
∎

Lemma A.6 (Universality).

Let
\Gamma\vDash\sigma
.
If
M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

then
M^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma\vDash\sigma
.
By
proposition

3.7
,
it suffices to show that
M^{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

implies
M^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Suppose that
M^{\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Let us write
|A|
for the size of the type
A
,
and
|\Gamma|
for the maximum size of a type occurring in
\Gamma
,

i.e.

|\Gamma|\,\mathrel{:=}\,\max\{|A|\ |\ \exists x.\,(x:A\in\Gamma)\}
.
We show that
M^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

by induction on the lexicographic measure
(|\Gamma|,n)
,
where
n
is the length of the reduction sequence
M^{\Gamma}\to_{w}^{n}\star
.
We consider two cases, depending on whether this reduction sequence is empty or
not,
i.e.

n=0
or
n>0
.

Empty (
n=0
).

Then
M^{\Gamma}=\star
.
We claim that
M=\star
.
Indeed, note that
M
cannot be a variable, because if

M=x
is a variable
then either
x:A\in\Gamma

and
M^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\neq\star
,
or
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
and
M^{\Gamma}=x\neq\star
.
Moreover, if
M
is not
\star
nor a variable, then

M^{\Gamma}\neq\star

(for example, if
M
is an application then

M^{\Gamma}
is also an application).
So we have that
M=\star
and it is immediate to conclude that

M^{\sigma}=\star\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Non-empty (
n=1+m
).

Since the reduction sequence
M^{\Gamma}\to_{w}^{n}\star
is non-empty,

M^{\Gamma}
has a weak head redex.
By
lemma

A.5
there are four cases:
either
M=\mathtt{W}[R]
where
R
is a redex,
or
M=\mathtt{W}[x\,N]
where
x^{\Gamma}
is an abstraction,
or
M=\mathtt{W}[x{?}N]
where
x^{\Gamma}=\star
,
or
M=\mathtt{W}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x]
where
x^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
.
We consider each of these four cases individually:

1.

If
M=\mathtt{W}[R]
where
R
is a redex:
consider the root reduction step
R\to_{w}S
.
First note that
R^{\Gamma}\to_{w}S^{\Gamma}

is also a root reduction step by
lemma

A.3
.
Since head reduction is deterministic (
lemma

A.3
),
the input reduction sequence must be of the form:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\Gamma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[R^{\Gamma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&\to_{w}&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[S^{\Gamma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}, using that $R^{\Gamma}\to_{w}S^{\Gamma}$}\\
&\to_{w}^{m}&\star\end{array}

The reduction sequence

\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[S^{\Gamma}]\to_{w}^{m}\star

is shorter than the original one (
m<n
).
We may apply the i.h., using the same environment
\Gamma
,
in such a way that the lexicographic measure decreases:

(|\Gamma|,n)>(|\Gamma|,m)
.
By i.h. we have that

\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[S^{\sigma}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
To conclude, note that

R^{\sigma}\to_{w}S^{\sigma}

is a root reduction step by
lemma

A.3
,
so:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\sigma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[R^{\sigma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&\to_{w}&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[S^{\sigma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}, using that $R^{\sigma}\to_{w}S^{\sigma}$}\\
&\twoheadrightarrow_{w}&\star&\text{as obtained from the i.h. }\end{array}

2.

If
M=\mathtt{W}[x\,N]

where
x^{\Gamma}
is an abstraction,

i.e.

x^{\Gamma}=\lambda y.\,P
.

First, we claim that
x:A\Rightarrow B\in\Gamma

for certain types
A,B
.
Indeed, if
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
then
x^{\Gamma}=x

is not an abstraction; so we must have that
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.
Moreover if
x:\mathbf{a}\in\Gamma
,
i.e.
if it is assigned an atomic type,
then
x^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
is not an abstraction;
so we must have that
x
occurs in
\Gamma
with a type which
is not an atomic type.
Thus
x
must occur in
\Gamma
with an arrow type.

Then the input reduction sequence must be of the form:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\Gamma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[x\,N]^{\Gamma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[x^{\Gamma}\,N^{\Gamma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}\,N^{\Gamma}]&\text{as $x:A\Rightarrow B\in\Gamma$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[(\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,N^{\Gamma}]\\
&\to_{w}&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\\
&\to_{w}^{m}&\star\end{array}

Consider the tail of the reduction sequence:

\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\to_{w}^{m}\star

By
lemma

A.2
, there exist
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 0
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}\to_{w}^{m_{1}}\star

and

\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]\to_{w}^{m_{2}}\star

and
m=1+m_{1}+m_{2}
.

Since
\Gamma\vDash\sigma
by hypothesis
and
x:A\Rightarrow B\in\Gamma
,
we know that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
By definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\cdot)
, we have:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\sigma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

Let
y
be a fresh variable, and consider
the metaterm
\mathtt{W}[y]
,
the environment
\Gamma_{1}:=\Gamma\cup\{y:B\}
,
and the substitution
\sigma_{1}:=\sigma[y\mapsto x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}]
.
Note that
\Gamma_{1}\vDash\sigma_{1}

because for
y:B\in\Gamma_{1}

we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(y^{\sigma_{1}})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
,
and for any variable other than
y
the property
follows from the fact that
\Gamma\vDash\sigma
.
Recall that
x:A\Rightarrow B\in\Gamma

so
|\Gamma|\geq|A\Rightarrow B|>|B|
,
which means that

|\Gamma_{1}|=|\Gamma,y:B|=\max\{|\Gamma|,|B|\}=|\Gamma|
.
Also, note that:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&\mathtt{W}[y]^{\Gamma_{1}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma_{1}}[y^{\Gamma_{1}}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[y^{\Gamma_{1}}]&\text{since $y$ is fresh for $\mathtt{W}$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}]&\text{by definition of $y^{\Gamma_{1}}$}\\
&\to_{w}^{m_{2}}&\star&\text{as already shown}\end{array}

Furthermore, note that the lexicographic measure decreases:

(|\Gamma|,n)=(|\Gamma_{1}|,n)>(|\Gamma_{1}|,m_{2})
,
so we may apply the i.h. to obtain that

\mathtt{W}[y]^{\sigma_{1}}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Hence:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}]\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[y^{\sigma_{1}}]&\text{by definition of $y^{\sigma_{1}}$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma_{1}}[y^{\sigma_{1}}]&\text{since $y$ is fresh for $\mathtt{W}$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[y]^{\sigma_{1}}&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&\twoheadrightarrow_{w}&\star&\text{as just shown}\end{array}

Recall that we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\Gamma}\to_{w}^{m_{1}}\star
.
Taking the environment
\Gamma
and the substitution
\sigma
,
we can note that the lexicographic measure decreases:

(|\Gamma|,n)>(|\Gamma|,m_{1})
,
so may apply the i.h. to obtain that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Now let
z
be a fresh variable, and consider
the metaterm
\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]
,
the environment
\Gamma_{2}:=\{z:A\}
,
and the substitution
\sigma_{2}:=[z\mapsto N^{\sigma}]
.
Note that
\Gamma_{2}\vDash\sigma_{2}

because
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
, as we have
just proved.
Moreover, we have that:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]^{\Gamma_{2}}\\
&=&(\mathtt{W}^{\sigma})^{\Gamma_{2}}[(x^{\sigma})^{\Gamma_{2}}\,z^{\Gamma_{2}}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z^{\Gamma_{2}}]&\text{since $z$ is fresh for $\mathtt{W},x,\sigma$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}]&\text{since $z:A\in\Gamma_{2}$}\\
&\twoheadrightarrow_{w}&\star&\text{as already shown}\end{array}

Let the length of this reduction sequence be called
n^{\prime}
.
Note that the lexicographic measure decreases:

(|\Gamma|,n)>(|\Gamma_{2}|,n^{\prime})

because the first component decreases,
i.e.

|\Gamma|\geq|A\Rightarrow B|>|A|=|\Gamma_{2}|
,
so we may apply the i.h. to conclude that

\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]^{\sigma_{2}}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Finally, note that:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\sigma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[x\,N]^{\sigma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,N^{\sigma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z^{\sigma_{2}}]&\text{by definition of $\sigma_{2}$}\\
&=&(\mathtt{W}^{\sigma})^{\sigma_{2}}[(x^{\sigma})^{\sigma_{2}}\,z^{\sigma_{2}}]&\text{since $z$ is fresh for $\mathtt{W},x,\sigma$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[x^{\sigma}\,z]^{\sigma_{2}}&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&\twoheadrightarrow_{w}&\star&\text{as just shown}\end{array}

3.

If
M=\mathtt{W}[x{?}N]

where
x^{\Gamma}=\star
:
we claim that this case is impossible.
Indeed, since
x^{\Gamma}=\star
,
we know that
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
,
but if
x:A\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)

then
x^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\neq\star
,
contradicting the fact that
x^{\Gamma}=\star
.

4.

If
M=\mathtt{W}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x]

where
x^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
:
then it must be the case that
x:\mathbf{a}\in\Gamma
.
Since head reduction is deterministic (
lemma

A.3
),
the input reduction sequence must be of the form:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\Gamma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x]^{\Gamma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\Gamma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}]&\text{since $x:\mathbf{a}\in\Gamma$}\\
&\to_{w}&\mathtt{W}^{\Gamma}[\star]\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[\star]^{\Gamma}&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&\to_{w}^{m}&\star\end{array}

Note that the lexicographic measure decreases:

(|\Gamma|,n)>(|\Gamma|,m)
,
so by i.h. we have that

\mathtt{W}[\star]^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Hence:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&M^{\sigma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x]^{\sigma}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\sigma}]&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}^{\sigma}[\star]&\text{because ${\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star$ by $\Gamma\vDash\sigma$}\\
&=&\mathtt{W}[\star]^{\sigma}&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subst_commute_wctx}}\\
&\twoheadrightarrow_{w}&\star&\text{as just shown}\end{array}

∎

A.3.
Minimal Logic: Completeness (
theorem

3.14
)

Remark A.7.

\lvert x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash A^{\prime}}=\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}
.

Lemma A.8.

If
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(M^{\downarrow})

then
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:B\vdash A}

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
M^{\downarrow}
.
∎

Lemma A.9 (Proof size of application to variables).

1.

Let
M^{\downarrow}
be a normal term.
Then the application
M^{\downarrow}\,x

\beta
-reduces to a
\beta
-normal form

N^{\downarrow}
in at most one step,
and
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.

2.

Let
M^{\downarrow}
be a normal term.
Then
M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}

\beta
-reduces to a
\beta
-normal form
N

in at most
n
steps,
and
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash B}
.

Proof.

The second item is immediate by induction on
n
, resorting to the first item
in each step.
To show the first item, proceed by induction on the shape of the
normal term
M^{\downarrow}
according to
definition

3.12
.

In the
abstraction
case,
we have that
M^{\downarrow}=\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}
.
Note that
M^{\downarrow}\,x=(\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow})\,x\to N^{\downarrow}

and:

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}=\lvert\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}

In the
neutral term
case,
we have that
M^{\downarrow}=y\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}
.
Take
N^{\downarrow}:=y\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\,x
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be the following conditions:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{P}&\equiv&\text{there exist $C,C_{1},\ldots,C_{n},D$}\\
&&\text{such that $y:C\in\Gamma$
and $C=(C_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots C_{n}\Rightarrow D)$}\\
\mathcal{Q}&\equiv&\text{there exist $C,C_{1},\ldots,C_{n},A^{\prime},B^{\prime}$}\\
&&\text{such that $y:C\in\Gamma$
and $C=(C_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots C_{n}\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime})$}\end{array}

If
\mathcal{Q}
does not hold, we are done,
because

\lvert y\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}=0
,
so

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\geq 0=\lvert y\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.

Assume that
\mathcal{Q}
holds, and note that
\mathcal{P}
also holds.
More precisely,
there exist
C,C_{1},\ldots,C_{n},A^{\prime},B^{\prime}

such that
y:C\in\Gamma

and
C=(C_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots C_{n}\Rightarrow A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime})
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\,\,}cll}&&\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\\
&=&\lvert y\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert A\Rightarrow B\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{1+\lvert A\rvert+\lvert B\rvert,1+\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&>&\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}+\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}+\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}+\lvert x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash A^{\prime}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash C_{i}}+\lvert x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash A^{\prime}}\,\,\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:size_weakening}}\\
&=&\lvert y\,M^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots M^{\downarrow}_{n}\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}\end{array}

∎

Lemma A.10 (Proof size of arguments).

Let
xM_{1}\ldots M_{n}
be a metaterm headed by a variable
and suppose that
x:(B_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow B_{n}\Rightarrow A)\in\Gamma
.
Then for all
1\leq i\leq n

we have that

\lvert xM_{1}\ldots M_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}>\lvert M_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B_{i}}
.

Proof.

Immediate, since by definition

\lvert xM_{1}\ldots M_{n}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert A\rvert+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert M_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B_{i}}>\lvert M_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B_{i}}
.
∎

Theorem A.11 (Completeness).

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
M
is good,
then its
\beta
-normal form is a term
M^{\downarrow}

such that
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\downarrow}
be the
\beta
-normal form of
M
, which is a normal term.
We proceed by induction on the natural number
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}
.
Without loss of generality, let us write

A=(A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a})
.
Note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be fresh variables,
and consider the metaterm
M\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}

and the environment
\Delta:=(\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n})
.
Note that
M^{\Gamma}=M^{\Delta}
because
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}

are fresh.
Then we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n})^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Delta}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{1}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A_{n}})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Let
N^{\downarrow}
be the
\beta
-normal form of
M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}
.
Then by confluence (
proposition

3.6
)
we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Note that
N^{\downarrow}
is pure and
that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash\mathbf{a}}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash\mathbf{a}}

holds by
lemma

A.9
.

Since
N^{\downarrow}
is a pure metaterm in
\beta
-normal form, we consider two
cases, depending on whether it is an abstraction or a neutral term.

First,
if
N^{\downarrow}
is an
abstraction
,

i.e.

N^{\downarrow}=\lambda y.\,P
,
then we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\lambda y.\,P^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda y.\,P)^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
which is impossible,
because the reducts of an abstraction are always abstractions,
so in particular
\lambda y.\,P^{\Delta}

cannot have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
as a reduct.

Second, if
N^{\downarrow}
is a
neutral term
,
then
N^{\downarrow}=x\,N_{1}\ldots N_{m}
,
and we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\Delta}\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x\,N_{1}\ldots N_{m})^{\Delta}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
We claim that
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
.
Indeed, if
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
, then
x^{\Delta}=x
,
and we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
which is impossible,
because the reducts of a metaterm headed by a variable
x

are always headed by
x
,
so in particular
x\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta}

cannot have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
as a reduct.

Then
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
. Suppose then that
x:B\in\Delta
.
Without loss of generality,
B
is of the form

B=(B_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow B_{k}\Rightarrow\mathbf{b})
.
For an arbitrary metaterm
M
,
and assuming
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(M)
,
let us write
A^{?}M
to abbreviate
\lambda x.\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}M)
.
Note that:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\Delta}\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\\
&\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&\star\end{array}

We first claim that
k=m
.
Let us discard the cases
k<m
and
k>m
:

The case
k<m
is impossible, because if
k<m
we obtain a contradiction.
We claim that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})

cannot reduce to
\star
.
To show this, we consider two cases:

1.

If for every index
1\leq i\leq m
we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
then:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}\,N_{k+1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})
.
The reducts of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}\,N_{k+1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta}

always have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}
at the head and exactly
m-k>0
arguments,
so the argument of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\cdot)
does not reduce to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
and the metaterm does not reduce to
\star
.

2.

Otherwise, let
1\leq i\leq m
be the least index such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}
does not reduce to
\star
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\\
\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}{?}(B_{i+1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}))\,N_{i+1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\end{array}

Since
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}
does not reduce to
\star
,
the unit eliminator

({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}{?}(\cdot))

does not reduce to a redex,
so the argument of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\cdot)
does not reduce to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
and the metaterm does not reduce to
\star
.

The case
k>m
is impossible, because if
k>m
we obtain a contradiction.
We claim that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})

cannot reduce to
\star
.
To show this, note first that
if there is an index
1\leq i\leq k
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}
does not reduce to
\star
,
the situation is similar as for
item

1
above.
If for every index
1\leq i\leq k
we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
then:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(B_{m+1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})

Note that the reducts of
B_{m+1}^{?}(\cdot)
always start with an abstraction,
so the argument of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\cdot)
does not reduce to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
and the metaterm does not reduce to
\star
.

Hence
k=m
.
If there is an index
1\leq i\leq k
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}
does not reduce to
\star
,
the situation is similar as for
item

1
above.
Hence
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

for every index
1\leq i\leq k
, and we have that:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((B_{1}^{?}\ldots B_{k}^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}})\,N_{1}^{\Delta}\ldots N_{m}^{\Delta})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}

so
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
which means that
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}
.

Recall that
N^{\downarrow}
is a pure metaterm in normal form,
and that
N^{\downarrow}=x\,N_{1}\ldots N_{k}
,
so
N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}
are also pure metaterms in normal form.
Note also that:

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash\mathbf{a}}>\lvert N_{i}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash B_{i}}

holds for all
1\leq i\leq k
, by resorting to
lemma

A.10
.
Since, for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
we know that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{i}^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
by i.h. we obtain that

\Delta\vdash N^{\downarrow}_{i}:B_{i}
.
Since
x:(B_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots\Rightarrow B_{k}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a})\in\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
,
we can derive the judgment

\Delta\vdash x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}:\mathbf{a}
.
Take
t:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}
.
Note that:

\begin{array}[]{llll}t&=&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}\\
&\mathrel{=_{\beta}}&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N_{1}\ldots N_{k}\\
&=&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,N^{\downarrow}\\
&\mathrel{=_{\beta}}&\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}&\text{as $M^{\downarrow}\,x_{1}\ldots x_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}N^{\downarrow}$}\\
&\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}&M^{\downarrow}&\text{using $n$ steps of $\eta$-reduction}\end{array}

Thus
t\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}M
.
By confluence of
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}

(
4
, Theorem 3.2.8)
, we have

t\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}M
.
Moreover, since
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
and
\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}
commute
(
4
, Lemma 3.3.8)

and
M^{\downarrow}
is in
\beta
-normal form,
the situation is
t\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}M
.

Finally, since
\Delta=(\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n})
,
we have that

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\,x\,N^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots N^{\downarrow}_{k}:A_{1}\Rightarrow\ldots A_{n}\Rightarrow\mathbf{a}
,
that is

\Gamma\vdash t:A
.
Since
t\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}
,
by subject reduction
(
3
, Proposition 1B.6)

we conclude that
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.
∎

Appendix B
Second-Order Logic — Properties of
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}

B.1.
Second-Order: Subcommutativity of Weak Head Reduction (
proposition

4.2
)

Proposition B.1.

Weak head reduction
\to_{w}
is subcommutative.

Proof.

Suppose that
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
and
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{3}
,
and let us show that there exists a metaterm
M_{4}

such that
M_{2}\to_{w}^{=}M_{4}
and
M_{3}\to_{w}^{=}M_{4}
.
We proceed by induction on
M_{1}
.

Note that the cases in which
M_{1}
is a
variable
(
M_{1}=x
),
a
term-abstraction
(
M_{1}=\lambda x.\,M^{\prime}_{1}
),
a
type-abstraction
(
M_{1}=\Lambda{a}.\,M^{\prime}_{1}
),
and a
success
(
M_{1}=\star
)
are impossible, because metaterms of these shapes
are
\to_{w}
-irreducible.

If
M_{1}
is a
term-application
,
M_{1}=N_{1}\,P_{1}
:
then there are three subcases, depending on whether the
steps
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
and
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{3}

are both at the root, both internal to
N_{1}
,
or one at the root and one internal:

1.

If both steps are at the root:
note that there is only one rewriting rule in
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
that involves
an application at the root.
Hence
N_{1}=\lambda x.\,N^{\prime}_{1}

and the two steps are of the form

(\lambda x.\,N^{\prime}_{1})\,P_{1}\to_{w}N^{\prime}_{1}\{x:=P_{1}\}
,
so
M_{2}=M_{3}=N^{\prime}_{1}\{x:=P_{1}\}

and we are done.

2.

If both steps are internal to
N_{1}
:
then the step
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}

is of the form
N_{1}\,P_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}\,P_{1}

with
N_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}
.
Similarly, the step
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}

is of the form
N_{1}\,P_{1}\to_{w}N_{3}\,P_{1}

with
N_{1}\to_{w}N_{3}
.
By i.h., there exists a term
N_{4}
such that

N_{2}\to_{w}^{=}N_{4}
and
N_{3}\to_{w}^{=}N_{4}
.
Taking
M_{4}:=N_{4}\,P_{1}

we have that

M_{2}=N_{2}\,P_{1}\to_{w}N_{4}\,P_{1}=M_{4}

and

M_{3}=N_{3}\,P_{1}\to_{w}N_{3}\,P_{1}=M_{4}
.

3.

If one step is at the rule, and one internal to
N_{1}
:
we claim that this case is impossible.
Without loss of generality,
suppose that the first step
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
is at the root
and the second step
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{3}
is internal to
N_{1}
.
Then
N_{1}=\lambda x.\,N^{\prime}_{1}

and the first step is of the form

M_{1}=(\lambda x.\,N^{\prime}_{1})\,P_{1}\to_{w}N^{\prime}_{1}\{x:=P_{1}\}=M_{2}
.
Then the second step is of the form

M_{1}=(\lambda x.\,N^{\prime}_{1})\,P_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}\,P_{1}

with
\lambda x.\,N^{\prime}_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}
,
but there are no rules that allow weak head reduction
\to_{w}

on a
\lambda
-abstraction.

If
M_{1}
is a
type-application
(
M_{1}=N_{1}\,A
)
or a
guard
(
M_{1}=N_{1}{?}N_{2}
),
the proof is similar to that of the term-abstraction.

If
M_{1}
is a
generator
,
M_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}
,
the proof is immediate because there is at most one way to reduce
M_{1}
.

If
M_{1}
is a
verifier
,
M_{1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{1}

there are four cases, depending on the shape of
A
:

1.

Type-variable (
A=a
):
Then the only way to reduce
M_{1}
is with a reduction internal to

N_{1}
and we conclude resorting to the i.h..

2.

Eigenvariable (
A=\mathbf{a}
):
Then there are two cases.
If
N_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
then
M_{1}
reduces at the root,
so
M_{1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\to_{w}\star

and this is the only way to reduce
M_{1}
.
Otherwise
N_{1}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
so both steps
M_{1}\to M_{2}
and
M_{1}\to M_{3}

must be internal to
N_{1}
and we conclude by
resorting to the i.h..

3.

Implication (
A=(B\Rightarrow C)
):
If both steps are at the root or both are internal to
N_{1}
,
it is easy to conclude,
similarly as in the case of the term-abstraction.
Without loss of generality, suppose that

M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
is at the root
and

M_{1}\to_{w}M_{3}
is internal to
N_{1}
.
Then the situation is the following,
where
N_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}
:

\textstyle{M_{1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})=M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{3}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A\Rightarrow B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A})=M_{4}}

4.

Universal quantification (
A=(\forall a.\,B)
):
If both steps are at the root or both are internal to
N_{1}
,
it is easy to conclude,
similarly as in the case of the term-abstraction.
Without loss of generality, suppose that

M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
is at the root
and

M_{1}\to_{w}M_{3}
is internal to
N_{1}
.
Then the situation is the following,
where
N_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}
:

\textstyle{M_{1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\forall a.\,B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N_{1}\,\mathbf{a})=M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{3}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\forall a.\,B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(N_{2}\,\mathbf{a})=M_{4}}

If
M_{1}
is a
fresh eigenvariable introduction

(
M_{1}=\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,N_{1})
,
then if both steps are at the root or both are internal to
N_{1}
,
it is easy to conclude, similarly as in the case of the term-abstraction.
Without loss of generality, suppose that

M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
is at the root
and

M_{1}\to_{w}M_{3}
is internal to
N_{1}
.
Then the situation is the following,
where
N_{1}\to_{w}N_{2}
:

\textstyle{M_{1}=\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{1}=M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{3}=\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{2}=M_{4}}

Note that the step on the top of the diagram is such that

\mathbf{a}\notin\mathsf{fv}(N_{1})

and reduction does not create free eigenvariables so
the fact that
\mathbf{a}\notin\mathsf{fv}(N_{2})

allows us to perform the step on the bottom of the diagram.
∎

B.2.
Second-Order: Confluence (
proposition

4.3
)

Definition B.2 (Simultaneous reduction).

The relation
M\rhd N
,
called
simultaneous reduction
,
is defined as follows between metaterms of
\lambda^{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
:

\frac{}{x\rhd x}{\texttt{var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}}{\lambda x.\,M\rhd\lambda x.\,M^{\prime}}{\texttt{lam}}

\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptN\rhd N^{\prime}}{M\,N\rhd M^{\prime}\,N^{\prime}}{\texttt{app1}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptN\rhd N^{\prime}}{(\lambda x.\,M)\,N\rhd M^{\prime}\{x:=N^{\prime}\}}{\texttt{app2}}

\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}}{\Lambda{a}.\,M\rhd\Lambda{a}.\,M^{\prime}}{\texttt{tlam}}

\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}}{M\,A\rhd M^{\prime}\,A}{\texttt{tapp1}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}}{(\Lambda{a}.\,M)\,A\rhd M^{\prime}\{a:=A\}}{\texttt{tapp2}}

\frac{}{\star\rhd\star}{\texttt{success}}

\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptN\rhd N^{\prime}}{M{?}N\rhd M^{\prime}{?}N^{\prime}}{\texttt{guard1}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}}{\star{?}M\rhd M^{\prime}}{\texttt{guard2}}

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}}{\texttt{g-refl}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A\Rightarrow B}\rhd\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}}{\texttt{g-imp}}

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\forall a.\,A}\rhd\Lambda{a}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}}{\texttt{g-all}}

\frac{M\rhd M^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\rhd{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\prime}}{\texttt{v-cong}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\rhd\star}{\texttt{v-eig}}
