{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B^{{\eta*}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}s^{{\eta*}\gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Let us write
\sigma\,\mathrel{:=}\,\tilde{\eta}[a\mapsto a]

be the type-substitution corresponding to the first projection
of
\eta
but fixing
a
,
and note that

(\forall a.\,B)^{\eta}=\forall a.\,B^{\sigma}

and

(\Lambda{a}.\,s)^{\eta}=\Lambda{a}.\,s^{\sigma}
.
Moreover, assume by
\alpha
-conversion that
a
does not occur
free in the image of
\gamma
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\forall a.\,B)^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{a}.\,s)^{\eta\gamma})\\
&=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\forall a.\,B^{\sigma}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{a}.\,s^{\sigma\gamma})\\
&\to_{w}&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B^{\sigma}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{a}.\,s^{\sigma\gamma})\,\mathbf{a})\\
&\to_{w}&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B^{\sigma\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{\sigma\gamma}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\})\\
&\to_{w}&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B^{{\eta*}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(s^{{\eta*}\gamma})\\
&\twoheadrightarrow_{w}&\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,\star\hskip 14.22636pt\text{since
${\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B^{{\eta*}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}s^{{\eta*}\gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star$
}\\
&\to_{w}&\star\end{array}

2.

To show the second condition,
let
C
be a type and
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{C}
,
and consider the candidate-assignment
{\eta*}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\eta[a\mapsto(C,X)]
.
Recall that
\Gamma\vDash^{\eta}\gamma
holds by hypothesis,
and observe that
\Gamma\vDash^{{\eta*}}\gamma
also holds,
because if
x:D\in\Gamma

then by hypothesis
we know that
x^{\gamma}\in\llbracket D\rrbracket\eta

and the application of the introduction rule for
\forall
ensures
that
a\notin\mathsf{fv}(D)
;
hence
\eta
and
{\eta*}
agree on the free variables of
D
,
so
x^{\gamma}\in\llbracket D\rrbracket{\eta*}

by irrelevance (
lemma

4.9
).
Applying the i.h., this implies that

s^{{\eta*}\gamma}\in\llbracket B\rrbracket{\eta*}
.
Note that we may assume by
\alpha
-conversion
that
a
does not occur free in the image of
\gamma
.
Moreover, consider the type-substitution
\sigma=\tilde{\eta}[a\mapsto a]

and note that

(\Lambda{a}.\,s)^{\eta}=\Lambda{a}.\,s^{\sigma}
.
Then we have that:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&(\Lambda{a}.\,s)^{\eta\gamma}\,C\\
&=&(\Lambda{a}.\,s^{\sigma\gamma})\,C\\
&\to_{w}&s^{\sigma\gamma}\{a:=C\}\\
&=&(s^{\sigma}\{a:=C\})^{\gamma}\\
&=&s^{{\eta*}\gamma}\end{array}

Since
\llbracket B\rrbracket{\eta*}
is a

B^{{\eta*}}
-verification candidate by
lemma

4.8
,
in particular it is closed by
\to_{w}
-convertibility,
and since we have that

(\Lambda{a}.\,s)^{\eta\gamma}\,C\twoheadrightarrow_{w}s^{{\eta*}\gamma}\in\llbracket B\rrbracket{\eta*}
,
we conclude that

(\Lambda{a}.\,s)^{\eta\gamma}\,C\in\llbracket B\rrbracket{\eta*}
.

Elimination of the second-order universal quantifier:

Let
t=s\,C

and let
B,a,C

be such that
A=B\{a:=C\}

and

\Gamma\vdash s\,C:B\{a:=C\}

is derived from

\Gamma\vdash s:\forall a.\,B
.
By i.h. we have that

s^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket\forall a.\,B\rrbracket\eta
.
Moreover, by definition of
\llbracket\forall a.\,B\rrbracket\eta
,
the fact that

s^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket\forall a.\,B\rrbracket\eta

entails that

s^{\eta\gamma}\,D\in\llbracket B\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(D,X)]

for every type
D
and every
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{D}
.
In particular, taking
D\,\mathrel{:=}\,C^{\eta}

and
X\,\mathrel{:=}\,\llbracket C\rrbracket\eta
,
which is a
C^{\eta}
-verification candidate by
lemma

4.8
,
we have that

s^{\eta\gamma}\,C^{\eta}\in\llbracket B\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(C^{\eta},\llbracket C\rrbracket\eta)]
.
Remark that

(s\,C)^{\eta\gamma}=s^{\eta\gamma}\,C^{\gamma}
.
Moreover, note that

\llbracket B\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(C^{\eta},\llbracket C\rrbracket\eta)]=\llbracket B\{a:=C\}\rrbracket\eta

by the substitution lemma (
lemma

4.10
).
Hence

(s\,C)^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket B\{a:=C\}\rrbracket\eta
,
as required.
∎

B.5.
Second-Order: Completeness (
theorem

4.14
)

Lemma B.19 (Characterization of normal forms).

A pure metaterm
M
is in
\beta
-normal form
if and only if it is a normal term, according to
the grammar of normal forms given in
section

4.2
.

Proof.

Both implications are routine by induction on
M
.
∎

Remark B.20 (Determinism of input matching).

The predicate
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A

depends functionally on
A
and
\vec{I}
.
More precisely,
if
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}_{1}\Rrightarrow A_{1}

and
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}_{2}\Rrightarrow A_{2}

hold, then
\vec{A}_{1}=\vec{A}_{2}
and
A_{1}=A_{2}
.

Remark B.21 (Input matching preserves the length).

If
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A
holds,
then
\lvert\vec{A}\rvert=\lvert\vec{I}\rvert
,

i.e.
they are lists of the same length.

Lemma B.22 (Input matching of a concatenation).

Let
A,A^{\prime}
be types and
\vec{A}
be a sequence of types.
Moreover, let
\vec{I}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}
be two sequences of inputs, and let us write

\vec{I}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}
for their concatenation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

A\,/\,(\vec{I}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}):\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

2.

There exist sequences of types
\vec{A}_{1},\vec{A}_{2}

and a type
C
such that

\vec{A}=\vec{A}_{1},\vec{A}_{2}

and

A\,/\,\vec{I}_{1}:\vec{A}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

and

A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{A}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on the length of
\vec{I}_{1}
.
We only prove the
(\Rightarrow)
direction; the
(\Leftarrow)
direction
is similar going backwards.
We consider three cases depending on whether
\vec{I}_{1}
is empty,
if it starts with a metaterm, or it starts with a type:

If
\vec{I}_{1}
is
empty
,
i.e.

\vec{I}_{1}=\varnothing
:
We know that
A\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
Take
\vec{A}_{1}:=\varnothing
,
\vec{A}_{2}:=\vec{A}
, and
A^{\prime\prime}:=A
.
Then
A\,/\,\vec{I}_{1}:\vec{A}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds because
A\,/\,\varnothing:\varnothing\Rrightarrow A
holds,
and
A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{A}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

holds because
A\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
holds.

If
\vec{I}_{1}

starts with a metaterm
,
i.e.

\vec{I}_{1}=(M,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1})
:
Suppose that
A\,/\,(M,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}):\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
The only way to derive this judgment is with

A=(B\Rightarrow C)

and
C\,/\,(\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}):\vec{C}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

and
\vec{A}=(B,\vec{C})
.
By i.h. there exist
\vec{C}_{1},\vec{C}_{2},A^{\prime\prime}

such that

\vec{C}=(\vec{C}_{1},\vec{C}_{2})

and

C\,/\,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1}:\vec{C}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

and

A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{C}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
Taking
\vec{A}_{1}:=(B,\vec{C}_{1})
and
\vec{A}_{2}:=\vec{C}_{2}
,
we have that

\vec{A}=(B,\vec{C})=(B,\vec{C}_{1},\vec{C}_{2})=(\vec{A}_{1},\vec{A}_{2})
.
Note that

A\,/\,\vec{I}_{1}:\vec{A}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds,
that is to say

(B\Rightarrow C)\,/\,(M,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1}):(B,\vec{C}_{1})\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds,
because

C\,/\,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1}:\vec{C}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds.
Moreover,
A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{A}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
holds
because
A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{C}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
holds.

If
\vec{I}_{1}

starts with a type
,
i.e.

\vec{I}_{1}=(B,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1})
:
Then suppose that
A\,/\,(B,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}):\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
The only way to derive this judgment is with
A=(\forall a.\,C)

and
C\{a:=B\}\,/\,(\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1},\vec{I}_{2}):\vec{C}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

and
\vec{A}=(B,\vec{C})
.
By i.h. there exist
\vec{C}_{1},\vec{C}_{2},A^{\prime\prime}
such that

\vec{C}=(\vec{C}_{1},\vec{C}_{2})

and

C\{a:=B\}\,/\,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1}:\vec{C}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

and

C^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{C}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
Taking
\vec{A}_{1}:=(B,\vec{C}_{1})
and
\vec{A}_{2}:=\vec{C}_{2}
,
we have that

\vec{A}=(B,\vec{C})=(B,\vec{C}_{1},\vec{C}_{2})=(\vec{A}_{1},\vec{A}_{2})
.
Note that

A\,/\,\vec{I}_{1}:\vec{A}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds,
that is to say

(\forall a.\,C)\,/\,(B,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1}):(B,\vec{C}_{1})\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds,
because

C\{a:=B\}\,/\,\vec{I}^{\prime}_{1}:\vec{C}_{1}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

holds.
Moreover,

A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{A}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

holds
because

A^{\prime\prime}\,/\,\vec{I}_{2}:\vec{C}_{2}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
holds.
∎

Definition B.23 (Coherence of inputs).

Let
I
and
J
be inputs,
and define
I\frown J
to hold if one of the
two following conditions holds:

1.

I
and
J
are both metaterms (but not necessarily equal).

2.

I
and
J
are the same types,

i.e.
there exists a type
A
such that
I=A=J
.

Moreover, if
\vec{I}=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})

and
\vec{J}=(J_{1},\ldots,J_{n})
,
we write
\vec{I}\frown\vec{J}

if
I_{i}\frown J_{i}
holds for every index
1\leq i\leq n
.

Remark B.24.

The question of whether the predicate
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
holds
does
not
depend on the metaterms appearing in the list of inputs
\vec{I}
.
More precisely, if
\vec{I}\frown\vec{J}

then
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

if and only if
A\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.

Remark B.25.

\lvert x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash A^{\prime}}=\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}
.

Lemma B.26.

If
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(M^{\downarrow})

then
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:B\vdash A}

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
M^{\downarrow}
.
∎

Lemma B.27 (Proof size of application to variable).

Let
M^{\downarrow}
be a normal term,
and let
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(M^{\downarrow})
.
Then there exists a
\beta
-normal form
N^{\downarrow}

such that
M^{\downarrow}\,x\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}N^{\downarrow}

and
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.

Proof.

We consider three cases depending on the shape of
M^{\downarrow}
,
according to
lemma

B.19
.

If
M^{\downarrow}
is a
head form
:
Let
M^{\downarrow}=H\,\vec{I}
where
\vec{I}=(I^{\downarrow}_{1},\ldots,I^{\downarrow}_{n})
.
Take
N^{\downarrow}:=H\,\vec{I}\,x
, and observe that it is in normal form.
Note that
M^{\downarrow}\,x=N^{\downarrow}
.
It suffices to show that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be the following conditions:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{P}&\equiv&\begin{array}[t]{l}\text{$H$ is a variable, $H=y$, and there exist $C,\vec{C},C^{\prime}$}\\
\text{such that
$y:C\in\Gamma$ and $C\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow C^{\prime}$.
}\end{array}\\
\mathcal{Q}&\equiv&\begin{array}[t]{l}\text{$H$ is a variable, $H=y$, and there exist $C,\vec{D},D^{\prime}$}\\
\text{such that
$y:C\in\Gamma$ and $C\,/\,(\vec{I},x):\vec{D}\Rrightarrow D^{\prime}$.
}\end{array}\end{array}

Note that if
\mathcal{Q}
does not hold then it is immediate to conclude,
since

\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}=\lvert y\,\vec{I}\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}=0
,
so

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\geq 0=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.
Therefore, we may assume that
\mathcal{Q}
holds.
We claim that then
\mathcal{P}
must also hold.
Indeed, since
\mathcal{Q}
holds
we have that
C\,/\,(\vec{I},x):\vec{D}\Rrightarrow D^{\prime}
,
which implies that
\mathcal{P}
holds by
lemma

B.22
.

Since
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
both hold,
the situation must be (by
lemma

B.22
and
remark

B.20
)
that
H=y
and
y:C\in\Gamma

and
C\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow(A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime})

and
(A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime})\,/\,x:A^{\prime}\Rrightarrow B^{\prime}
,
where moreover we know that
\vec{C}
is of the form

\vec{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{n})

by
remark

B.21
.
Hence:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\\
&=&\lvert y\,\vec{I}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert A\Rightarrow B\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert A\Rightarrow B\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash C_{i}}\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:f:size_weakening}}\\
&=&\min\{1+\lvert A\rvert+\lvert B\rvert,1+\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert A\rvert,\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\}+\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash C_{i}}+\lvert x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash A^{\prime}}\\
&=&\lvert y\,\vec{I}\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}\\
&=&\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}\end{array}

If
M^{\downarrow}
is a
term-abstraction
:
Let
M^{\downarrow}=\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}
.
Note that
M^{\downarrow}\,x\to N^{\downarrow}
in exactly one step.
Moreover

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}=\lvert\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.

If
M^{\downarrow}
is a
type-abstraction
:
Let
M^{\downarrow}=\Lambda{a}.\,P^{\downarrow}
.
Take
N^{\downarrow}:=(\Lambda{a}.\,P^{\downarrow})\,a
, and observe that it is in normal form.
Note that
M^{\downarrow}\,a=N^{\downarrow}
.
Moreover

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}=\lvert\Lambda{a}.\,P^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A\Rightarrow B}=0=\lvert(\Lambda{a}.\,P^{\downarrow})\,x\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:A\vdash B}
.
∎

Lemma B.28 (Substitution by eigenvariable).

1.

If

A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}

then

A\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\,/\,\vec{I}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}:\vec{A}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}
.

2.

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert_{\Gamma\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\vdash A\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}

Proof.

The first item is straightforward by induction on the derivation of the
judgment
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow A^{\prime}
.
The second item is straightforward by induction on
M^{\downarrow}
,
considering each case in
definition

4.13

and resorting to the first item when
M^{\downarrow}
is of the form
xI^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots I^{\downarrow}_{n}
.
∎

Lemma B.29 (Proof size of application to type-variable).

Let
M^{\downarrow}
be a normal term,
and suppose that
a,\mathbf{a}\notin\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)
.
Then there exists a
\beta
-normal form
N^{\downarrow}

such that
M^{\downarrow}\,\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\beta}}N^{\downarrow}
,
and
\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}
.

Proof.

We consider three cases depending on the shape of
M^{\downarrow}
,
according to
lemma

B.19
.

If
M^{\downarrow}
is a
head form
:
Let
M^{\downarrow}=H\,\vec{I}

where
\vec{I}=(I^{\downarrow}_{1},\ldots,I^{\downarrow}_{n})
.
Take
N^{\downarrow}:=H\,\vec{I}\,\mathbf{a}
, and observe that it is in normal form.
Then we have that
M^{\downarrow}\,\mathbf{a}=N^{\downarrow}
,
and it suffices to show that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be the following conditions:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{P}&\equiv&\begin{array}[t]{l}\text{$H$ is a variable, $H=x$, and there exist $C,\vec{C},C^{\prime}$}\\
\text{such that
$x:C\in\Gamma$ and $C\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow C^{\prime}$.
}\end{array}\\
\mathcal{Q}&\equiv&\begin{array}[t]{l}\text{$H$ is a variable, $H=x$, and there exist $C,\vec{D},D^{\prime}$}\\
\text{such that
$x:C\in\Gamma$ and $C\,/\,(\vec{I},\mathbf{a}):\vec{D}\Rrightarrow D^{\prime}$.
}\end{array}\end{array}

Note that if
\mathcal{Q}
does not hold then it is immediate to conclude,
since

\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}=\lvert x\,\vec{I}\,\mathbf{a}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}=0
,
so

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\geq 0=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}
.
Therefore, we may assume that
\mathcal{Q}
holds.
We claim that then
\mathcal{P}
must also hold.
Indeed, since
\mathcal{Q}
holds
we have that
C\,/\,(\vec{I},\mathbf{a}):\vec{D}\Rrightarrow D^{\prime}
,
which implies that
\mathcal{P}
holds by
lemma

B.22
.

Since
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
both hold,
the situation must be (by
lemma

B.22
and
remark

B.20
)
that
H=y
and
y:C\in\Gamma

and
C\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow(\forall a.\,B^{\prime})

and
(\forall a.\,B^{\prime})\,/\,\mathbf{a}:\mathbf{a}\Rrightarrow B^{\prime}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}
,
where moreover we know that
\vec{C}
is of the form

\vec{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{n})

by
remark

B.21
.
Hence:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\\
&=&\lvert x\,\vec{I}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert\forall a.\,B\rvert,\lvert\forall a.\,B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{1+\lvert B\rvert,1+\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&>&\min\{\lvert B\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}\\
&=&\min\{\lvert B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert,\lvert B^{\prime}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I^{\downarrow}_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash C_{i}}+\lvert\mathbf{a}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{a}}\\
&=&\lvert x\,\vec{I}\,\mathbf{a}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}\\
&=&\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}\end{array}

If
M^{\downarrow}
is a
term-abstraction
:
Let
M^{\downarrow}=\lambda x.\,P^{\downarrow}
.
Take
N^{\downarrow}:=(\lambda x.\,P^{\downarrow})\,\mathbf{a}

and observe that it is in normal form.
Note that
M^{\downarrow}\,x=N^{\downarrow}
.
Then

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall x.\,B}=\lvert\lambda x.\,P^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall x.\,B}=0=\lvert(\lambda x.\,P^{\downarrow})\,\mathbf{a}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}
.

If
M^{\downarrow}
is a
type-abstraction
:
Let
M^{\downarrow}=\Lambda{a}.\,P^{\downarrow}
.
Take
N^{\downarrow}:=P^{\downarrow}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}
,
where we remark that
P^{\downarrow}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}
is in normal form
because
P^{\downarrow}
is pure.
Note that
M^{\downarrow}\,\mathbf{a}\to P^{\downarrow}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}=N^{\downarrow}

in exactly one step.
Then

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}=\lvert\Lambda{a}.\,P^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}=\lvert P^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B}\,=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:f:subst_by_teig}}}\,\lvert P^{\downarrow}\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}
.
∎

Lemma B.30 (Proof size of inputs).

Let
\vec{I}=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})
be a sequence of normal inputs,
and let
\vec{A}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
be a sequence of types.
Suppose that
x:A\in\Gamma

and that
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
.
Then for every index
1\leq i\leq n

we have that
\lvert x\,\vec{I}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{a}}>\lvert I_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{i}}
.

Proof.

Immediate, since by definition:

\lvert x\,\vec{I}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{a}}=\lvert\mathbf{a}\rvert+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert I_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{i}}>\lvert I_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A_{i}}

∎

Lemma B.31 (Generator matching).

Let
\vec{I}=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})
be a sequence of inputs.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\,\vec{I})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

then there exists a sequence of types
\vec{A}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})

such that
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
.
Moreover, for every index
1\leq i\leq n

such that the input
I_{i}
is a metaterm,
we have that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}I_{i}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the number of inputs
n
,

i.e.
the length of
\vec{I}
.
We consider four cases, depending on the shape of
A
:

If
A
is a
type-variable
,
i.e.

A=b
:
We claim that this case is impossible.
Indeed, we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{b}\,\vec{I})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
But the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{b}\,\vec{I})

are of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{b}\,\vec{I}^{\prime})
,
which is a contradiction.
Observe, in the particular case when
n=0
,
that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{b}
is irreducible
because
b
is not an eigenvariable, so
b\neq\mathbf{a}
.

If
A
is a
eigenvariable
,
i.e.

A=\mathbf{b}
:
There are two cases, depending on whether
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}

or
\mathbf{a}\neq\mathbf{b}
.
The proof when
\mathbf{a}\neq\mathbf{b}
is similar to the case
in which
A
is an eigenvariable,

i.e.
it is impossible to construct a reduction

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{b}}\,\vec{I})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
If
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}
,
then we claim that
n=0
.
Indeed, if
n>0
,
all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,\vec{I})

are of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\,\text{...})
,
which is a contradiction.
Hence
n=0

and we have that
\mathbf{a}\,/\,\varnothing:\varnothing\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
.

If
A
is an
implication
,
i.e.

A=(B\Rightarrow C)
:
Then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\Rightarrow C}\,\vec{I})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\Rightarrow C}\,\vec{I})\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,\vec{I})

so by confluence (
proposition

4.3
)
we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,\vec{I})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
We claim that
\vec{I}
must be of the form
\vec{I}=(M,\vec{J})
where
the first element
M
is a metaterm, and
\vec{J}
is the tail.
Indeed, note that
\vec{I}
cannot be empty
because we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but this is impossible because all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})

are of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,\text{...})
, which is a contradiction.
Moreover,
\vec{I}
cannot be of the form
\vec{I}=(D,\vec{J})

where
D
is a type, because we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,D\,\vec{J})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but there are no rules to contract a pseudo-redex of the form

(\lambda x.\,\text{...})\,D
,
so all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,D\,\vec{J})

are of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,\text{...})\,D\,\text{...})
,
which is a contradiction.

Therefore,
\vec{I}=(M,\vec{J})
where
M
is a metaterm,
and we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}x{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,M\,\vec{J})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,\vec{J})

so by confluence (
proposition

4.3
) we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C})\,\vec{J})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Then we necessarily have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{C}\,\vec{J})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Since
\lvert\vec{I}\rvert=1+\lvert\vec{J}\rvert
,
we may resort to the i.h., obtaining that there exists a sequence of types
\vec{C}

such that
C\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}

and such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}J_{i}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

holds whenever
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
J_{i}
is a metaterm.
To conclude, take
\vec{A}:=(B,\vec{C})

where
B
is the first element and
\vec{C}
is the tail.
Observe that
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
holds
because it means that

(B\Rightarrow C)\,/\,(M,\vec{J}):(B,\vec{C})\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}

which in turn holds by definition,
because we have that
C\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{C}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
.
Moreover, the condition
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}I_{i}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

holds whenever
1\leq i\leq n
and
I_{i}
is a metaterm.
Indeed, when
i=1
this corresponds to the fact that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
and when
i>1
this follows from the i.h..

If
A
is a
universal quantification
,
i.e.

A=(\forall b.\,B)
:
Then we have that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\forall b.\,B}\,\vec{I}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
and we can note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\forall b.\,B}\,\vec{I}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,\vec{I})

so by confluence (
proposition

4.3
) we have that also

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,\vec{I})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
We claim that
\vec{I}
must be of the form
\vec{I}=(C,\vec{J})
where
the first element
C
is a type, and
\vec{J}
is the tail.
Indeed, note that
\vec{I}
cannot be empty because we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but this is impossible because all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})

are of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{b}.\,\text{...})
,
which is a contradiction.
Moreover,
\vec{I}
cannot be of the form
\vec{I}=(M,\vec{J})

where
M
is a metaterm, because we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,M\,\vec{J})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

but there are no rules to contract a pseudo-redex of the form

(\Lambda{b}.\,\text{...})\,M
,
so all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,M\,\vec{J})

are of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{b}.\,\text{...})\,M^{\prime}\,\text{...})
,
which is a contradiction.

Therefore,
\vec{I}=(C,\vec{J})
where
C
is a type,
and we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\Lambda{b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\,C\,\vec{J})\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\{b:=C\}}\,\vec{J})

so by confluence (
proposition

4.3
) we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B\{b:=C\}}\,\vec{J})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Since
\lvert\vec{I}\rvert=1+\lvert\vec{J}\rvert
, we may resort to the i.h.,
obtaining that there exists a sequence of types
\vec{D}

such that
B\{b:=C\}\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{D}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
,
and such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}D_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}J_{i}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

holds whenever
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
J_{i}
is a metaterm.
To conclude, take
\vec{A}:=(C,\vec{D})

where
C
is the first element and
\vec{D}
is the tail.
Observe that
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
holds
because it means that

(\forall b.\,B)\,/\,(C,\vec{J}):(C,\vec{D})\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}

which in turn holds by definition,
because we have that

B\{b:=C\}\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{D}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
.
Moreover, the condition
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}I_{i}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

holds whenever
1\leq i\leq n
and
I_{i}
is a metaterm,
which follows directly from the i.h..
∎

Lemma B.32 (Reconstruction of typing derivation).

Let
\vec{I}=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})
be a sequence of inputs such that all metaterms appearing in the sequence are pure,
and let
\vec{A}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
be a sequence of types.
Suppose that
\Gamma\vdash t:A

and that
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow B
.
Suppose moreover that
\Gamma\vdash I_{i}:A_{i}

holds whenever
1\leq i\leq n
and
I_{i}
is a metaterm.
Then
\Gamma\vdash t\,\vec{I}:B
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the judgment
A\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{A}\Rrightarrow B
:

1.

Suppose that the judgment is of the form
A\,/\,\varnothing:\varnothing\Rrightarrow A
,
where
\vec{I}=\varnothing
and
\vec{A}=\varnothing

and
B=A
.
Then immediately we have that
\Gamma\vdash t:A
holds by hypothesis.

2.

Suppose that the judgment
(C\Rightarrow D)\,/\,(s,\vec{J}):(C,\vec{D})\Rrightarrow B

is derived from
D\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{D}\Rrightarrow B
,
where
A=(C\Rightarrow D)

and
\vec{I}=(s,\vec{J})
and
\vec{A}=(C,\vec{D})
.
Then we know by hypothesis that
\Gamma\vdash t:C\Rightarrow D

and also that
\Gamma\vdash s:C
,
so by the elimination of the implication we have
\Gamma\vdash t\,s:D
.
Applying the i.h. on the judgments
\Gamma\vdash t\,s:D

and
D\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{D}\Rrightarrow B
,
we obtain that
\Gamma\vdash t\,s\,\vec{J}:B
,
that is,
\Gamma\vdash t\,\vec{I}:B
.

3.

Suppose that the judgment
(\forall a.\,C)\,/\,(D,\vec{J}):(D,\vec{E})\Rrightarrow B

is derived from
C\{a:=D\}\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{E}\Rrightarrow B
,
where
A=(\forall a.\,C)

and
\vec{I}=(D,\vec{J})

and
\vec{A}=(D,\vec{E})
.
Then we know by hypothesis that
\Gamma\vdash t:\forall a.\,C

so by the elimination of the second-order universal quantifier we have

\Gamma\vdash t\,D:C\{a:=D\}
.
Applying the i.h. on the judgments
\Gamma\vdash t\,D:C\{a:=D\}

and
C\{a:=D\}\,/\,\vec{J}:\vec{E}\Rrightarrow B
,
we obtain that
\Gamma\vdash t\,D\,\vec{J}:B
,
that is,
\Gamma\vdash t\,\vec{I}:B
.

∎

Theorem B.33 (Completeness).

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

and
M
is good,
then its
\beta
-normal form is a term
M^{\downarrow}
such
that
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:A
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\downarrow}
denote the
\beta
-normal form of
M
and proceed by induction
on the lexicographic pair
(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A},\lvert A\rvert)
,
where
\lvert A\rvert
denotes the size of
A
.
We consider four cases, depending on the shape of
A
.

If
A
is an
implication
,
i.e.

A=(B\Rightarrow C)
:
Note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\Rightarrow C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})
,
so by confluence (
proposition

4.3
) we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Let
x
be a fresh variable,
consider the environment
\Delta:=(\Gamma,x:B)
,
and the metaterm
M\,x
,
and note by
lemma

B.27

that there exists a pure metaterm
N^{\downarrow}
in
\beta
-normal form
such that
M\,x\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}M^{\downarrow}\,x\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}N^{\downarrow}
,
and

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\Rightarrow C}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma,x:B\vdash C}=\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash C}
.
We claim that we are now in a position to apply the i.h. on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta})
:

•

First, from the fact that
M\,x\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}N^{\downarrow}
,
we obtain that
(M\,x)^{\Delta}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta}
,
and since we know that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((M\,x)^{\Delta})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((M\,x)^{\Gamma,x:B})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
by confluence (
proposition

4.3
)
we obtain that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((N^{\downarrow})^{\Delta})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}C{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma,x:C})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

•

Second, note that the pair

(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A},\lvert A\rvert)=(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A},\lvert B\Rightarrow C\rvert)

is strictly greater than

(\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash C},\lvert C\rvert)

according to the lexicographic order,
because we have already shown that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Delta\vdash C}

and moreover

\lvert B\Rightarrow C\rvert>\lvert C\rvert
.

Hence by i.h. we obtain that
\Delta\vdash N^{\downarrow}:C
.
This means that
\Gamma,x:B\vdash N^{\downarrow}:C
,
so using the introduction rule for the implication
we have that
\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}:B\Rightarrow C
.
To conclude,
note that

\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}\lambda x.\,M^{\downarrow}\,x\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}
.
Since
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
and
\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}
commute, and
M^{\downarrow}

is in
\beta
-normal form, we have that

\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}
.
Hence, by subject reduction
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:B\Rightarrow C
.

If
A
is a
universal quantification
,
i.e.

A=(\forall a.\,B)
:
Assume by
\alpha
-conversion that
a\notin\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)
.
Note that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\forall a.\,B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\to\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{a})
,
where
\mathbf{a}
is assumed to be fresh in the sense that
\mathbf{a}\notin\mathsf{fv}(B,M,\Gamma)
,
so by confluence (
proposition

4.3
) we have that

\nu{\mathbf{a}}.\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{a})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
This in turn implies that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{a})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Consider the metaterm
M\,\mathbf{a}
,
and note by
lemma

B.29

that there exists a pure metaterm
N^{\downarrow}
in
\beta
-normal form
such that
M\,\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}M^{\downarrow}\,\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}N^{\downarrow}
,
and

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A}=\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}
.
We claim that we are now in a position to apply the i.h. on

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma})
.

•

First, from the fact that
M\,\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}N^{\downarrow}

we obtain that
(M\,\mathbf{a})^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}(N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma}
,
and since we know that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((M\,\mathbf{a})^{\Gamma})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(M^{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{a})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
, by confluence (
proposition

4.3
)
we obtain that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

•

Second, note that the pair

(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash A},\lvert A\rvert)=(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B},\lvert\forall a.\,B\rvert)

is strictly greater than the pair

(\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}},\lvert A\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert)

according to the lexicographic order,
because we have already shown that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\forall a.\,B}\geq\lvert N^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}}

and moreover

\lvert\forall a.\,B\rvert>\lvert B\rvert=\lvert B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}\rvert
.

Hence by i.h. we obtain that

\Gamma\vdash N^{\downarrow}:B\{a:=\mathbf{a}\}
.
Since we know that
a\notin\mathsf{fv}(\Gamma)
,
renaming
\mathbf{a}
by
a
, this means that

\Gamma\vdash N^{\downarrow}\{\mathbf{a}:=a\}:B
,
and using the introduction rule for the second-order universal quantifier
we have that
\Gamma\vdash\Lambda{a}.\,N^{\downarrow}\{\mathbf{a}:=a\}:\forall a.\,B
.
To conclude,
note that

\Lambda{a}.\,N\{\mathbf{a}:=a\}\mathrel{\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta}}\Lambda{a}.\,M^{\downarrow}\,a\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}
.
Since
\mathrel{\to_{\beta}}
and
\mathrel{\to_{\eta}}
commute, and
M^{\downarrow}

is in
\beta
-normal form, we have that

\Lambda{a}.\,N^{\downarrow}\{\mathbf{a}:=a\}\mathrel{\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}_{\eta}}M^{\downarrow}
.
Hence, by subject reduction
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:\forall a.\,B
.

If
A
is a
type-variable
,
i.e.

A=a
:
We claim that this case is impossible.
Indeed, by hypothesis,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but the outermost
a
-verifier is persistent,

i.e.
all the reducts of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}

are of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}\text{...}
, which is a contradiction.

If
A
is an
eigenvariable
,
A=\mathbf{a}
:
Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star

so by confluence

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((M^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
According to the characterization of normal forms given by
lemma

B.19
,

M^{\downarrow}
might be of three possible shapes,
namely either
M^{\downarrow}
is a term-abstraction (
M^{\downarrow}=\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow}
)
or a type-abstraction (
M^{\downarrow}=\Lambda{a}.\,N^{\downarrow}
)
or a neutral term (
M^{\downarrow}=H\,I^{\downarrow}_{1}\ldots I^{\downarrow}_{n}
).
We claim that
M^{\downarrow}
cannot be a term-abstraction,
as this implies that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,((N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma}))\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but there are no rewriting rules that allow forming a redex that involves
the outermost
\mathbf{a}
-verifier and the term-abstraction,
so all the reducts of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,((N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma}))

are of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\lambda x.\,\text{...})
,
which is a contradiction.
Similarly,
M^{\downarrow}
cannot be a type-abstraction,
as we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(\Lambda{a}.\,((N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma}))\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but this is impossible.

Therefore,
M^{\downarrow}
must be a neutral term,

i.e.

M^{\downarrow}=H\,\vec{I}
,
where
H
is a head form and
\vec{I}=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{n})
is a sequence of inputs
11
11

11

Note that
each input
I_{i}
is a normal input; we write just
I_{i}
rather than
the more explicit
I^{\downarrow}_{i}
to alleviate the notation.
.
Again, we have three possibilities,
either
H
is formed by a term-abstraction and a type-application (
H=(\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow})\,A
),
or by a type-abstraction and a term-application (
H=(\Lambda{a}.\,N^{\downarrow})\,P^{\downarrow}
),
or
H
is a variable (
H=x
).
We claim that
H
must be a variable.
Indeed, the case
H=(\lambda x.\,N^{\downarrow})\,A

is impossible, as we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,(N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma})\,A\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but there are no rewriting rules that allow contracting a
pseudo-redex of the form
(\lambda x.\,\text{...})\,A
,
so all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,(N^{\downarrow})^{\Gamma})\,A\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma})

must necessarily be of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}((\lambda x.\,\text{...})\,A\,\text{...})
,
which is a contradiction.
Similarly, the case
H=(\Lambda{a}.\,N^{\downarrow})\,P^{\downarrow}

is impossible.

Therefore,
H
must be a a variable,
and
M^{\downarrow}=x\,\vec{I}
. Note that we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x^{\Gamma}\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
We claim that
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.
Indeed, if
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)

we would have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
,
but all the reducts of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma})

must be of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}(x\,\text{...})
, a contradiction.
Hence there exists a type
B

such that
x:B\in\Gamma
,
and we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}\mathbf{a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{B}\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
By
lemma

B.31

this implies that there exists a sequence of types
\vec{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})

such that

B\,/\,\vec{I}^{\Gamma}:\vec{B}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}

and such that
for every index
1\leq i\leq n

such that the input
I_{i}^{\Gamma}
is a metaterm we have that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}B_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}I_{i}^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
Remark, for each index
1\leq i\leq n
, that

I_{i}^{\Gamma}
is a metaterm if and only if
I_{i}
is a metaterm.
Now note that

B\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{B}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}

holds by
remark

B.24

since
\vec{I}\frown\vec{I}^{\Gamma}
.
Hence, for every index
1\leq i\leq n
such that
I_{i}
is a metaterm we have that

\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{a}}=\lvert x\,\vec{I}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{a}}>\lvert I_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B_{i}}

by
lemma

B.30
.
In particular, the pair

(\lvert M^{\downarrow}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{a}},\lvert\mathbf{a}\rvert)

is strictly greater than the pair

(\lvert I_{i}\rvert_{\Gamma\vdash B_{i}},\lvert B_{i}\rvert)

according to the lexicographic order,
because the first component strictly decreases.

We may apply the i.h. to obtain that
\Gamma\vdash I^{\downarrow}_{i}:B_{i}

holds for every
1\leq i\leq n
such that
I_{i}
is a metaterm.
Observe that
I_{i}
must be in
\beta
-normal form
because
M^{\downarrow}
is a
\beta
-normal form and
M^{\downarrow}=x\vec{I}
,
so
I^{\downarrow}_{i}=I_{i}
.
Finally, since we have
B\,/\,\vec{I}:\vec{B}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{a}
,
and
\Gamma\vdash x:B
(since
x:B\in\Gamma
),
and
\Gamma\vdash I_{i}:B_{i}

holds for each metaterm in the sequence
\vec{I}
,
by
lemma

B.32

we conclude that
\Gamma\vdash x\,\vec{I}:\mathbf{a}
.
This means that
\Gamma\vdash M^{\downarrow}:\mathbf{a}
, as required.
∎

Appendix C
Second-Order Logic — Properties of
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}

C.1.
Higher-Order: Soundness (
theorem

5.2
)

In this section, we show that
{\lambda}^{\mathsf{F\omega}}
is sound with respect to the
epistemic realizability semantics induced by
{\lambda^{\mathsf{F\omega}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

A straightforward observation is that types have unique kinds under the global
kind-assignment,
i.e.
if
\Xi\vdash A:K_{1}
and
\Xi\vdash A:K_{2}

then
K_{1}=K_{2}
.

Lemma C.1 (Reduction preserves well-kindedness).

If
\mathtt{wk}(M)
and
M\to M^{\prime}

then
\mathtt{wk}(M^{\prime})
.

Definition C.2 (Sets of valid expressions).

We write
\mathbb{K}
for the set of all kinds.
A type is
ground
if it has no free type-variables (but note that it may have free eigenvariables).
We write
\mathbb{T}
for the set of
valid type/kind pairs
,
defined as
\mathbb{T}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\{(A,K)\ |\ \Xi\vdash A:K\}
.
The subset of
valid ground type/kind pairs

is defined by

\mathbb{T}^{0}:=\{(A,K)\ |\ A\text{ is ground}\land(A,K)\in\mathbb{T}\}
.
We write
\mathbb{T}_{K}
for the set of
valid types of kind
K
,
defined as
\mathbb{T}_{K}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\{A\ |\ \Xi\vdash A:K\}
.
The subset of
valid ground types of kind
K

is defined by
\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\{A\ |\ A\text{ is ground}\land\Xi\vdash A:K\}
.
Sometimes we write “
A:K
” instead of “
(A,K)
” for
elements of
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}^{0}
.
We write
\mathbb{M}
for the set of all well-kinded metaterms,

i.e.

\{M\ |\ \mathtt{wk}(M)\}
.

Definition C.3 (Dependent function space).

If
I
is a set of indices and
(J_{i})_{i\in I}
is an indexed family of sets,
we write
\prod_{i\in I}J_{i}

for the set of all functions
f:I\to\cup_{i\in I}J_{i}

such that
f(i)\in J_{i}
.

Sometimes we implicitly use an uncurried notation for functions;
so for example we write
f:\prod_{i\in I}J_{i}\to K_{i}

and
f(i,j)=k_{i,j}

for the function
f
such that

f(i)
is again a function
f(i):J_{i}\to K_{i}
for every
i\in I
,
and such that

f(i)(j)=k_{i,j}
holds for each
i\in I
and each
j\in J_{i}
.

Definition C.4 (Candidate-families).

A family of sets
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}

indexed by valid ground type/kind pairs
is a
candidate-family
if the following conditions hold:

1.

Stability under
\beta
-conversion.

For all
K\in\mathbb{K}

and all ground
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}
,
if
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}

then
\mathcal{VC}_{A_{1}:K}=\mathcal{VC}_{A_{2}:K}
.

2.

Inhabited.

For every ground
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}
there is an element
\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis\in\mathcal{VC}_{A:K}
.
Moreover, if
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}
and
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}

then
\llparenthesis A_{1}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A_{2}\rrparenthesis
.

3.

Candidate condition for base kinds.

For every base kind
\kappa
and every ground type
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{\kappa}

we have that
\mathcal{VC}_{A:\kappa}
is a singleton.
We write
\diamondsuit
for its unique element, so that

\mathcal{VC}_{A:\kappa}=\{\diamondsuit\}
.

4.

Candidate condition for propositions.

For every ground proposition
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{\mathtt{Prop}}
,
we have that
\mathcal{VC}_{A:\mathtt{Prop}}
is a set of sets of well-kinded metaterms,
that is,
\mathcal{VC}_{A:\mathtt{Prop}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{M})
.
Moreover, for every
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{A:\mathtt{Prop}}
we have that:

4.1

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\in X

4.2

If
M\in X
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

4.3

If
M_{1},M_{2}
are metaterms such that
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}

then
M_{1}\in X
if and only if
M_{2}\in X
.

5.

Candidate condition for higher-kinded types.

For all
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathbb{K}

and every ground
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}\to K_{2}}
,
we have that

\mathcal{VC}_{A:K_{1}\to K_{2}}
is the set of all functions
f

such that:

f:\prod_{B\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{B:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{AB:K_{2}}

and such that
for every
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}
,
and every
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{B:K_{1}}

if
B_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}B_{2}

then
f(B_{1},X)=f(B_{2},X)
.

Lemma C.5 (Correctness).

For any ground type
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{\mathtt{Prop}}

we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.

Proof.

By induction on the size of
A
.
Similar to the proof for the second-order case (
lemma

4.5
).
∎

Definition C.6 (Candidate-assignments).

Let
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family.
A
\mathcal{F}
-candidate-assignment
is a function
\eta

mapping each type-variable
a

to a pair
(A,X)
where,
if we let
K:=\Xi(a)

(that is,
K
is the kind of
a
in the global kind-assignment
\Xi
),
then we have that
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}
is a ground type and
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{A:K}
.
Usually the ambient candidate-family
\mathcal{F}
is clear from the context, and we
speak just of candidate-assignments, rather than of
\mathcal{F}
-candidate-assignments.
We write
\tilde{\eta}
for the
first projection
of
\eta
,
defined as the type-substitution such that

\tilde{\eta}(a)=A
whenever
\eta(a)=(A,X)
.
By abuse of notation, sometimes we write
\eta
for the first projection of
the candidate-assignment
\eta
instead of
\tilde{\eta}
, if there is little danger of confusion.
If
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are candidate-assignments,
we write
\eta_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta_{2}

if, for every type-variable
a
,
letting
\eta_{1}(a)=(A_{1},X_{1})

and
\eta_{2}(a)=(A_{2},X_{2})
,
we have that
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}
and
X_{1}=X_{2}
.

Definition C.7 (Candidate interpretation of types).

Let
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family.
For every valid type/kind pair
A:K\in\mathbb{T}

and every candidate-assignment
\eta

we define the interpretation
\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta

by recursion on
A
according to the equations below.
The construction is well-defined in the sense that
\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta\in\mathcal{VC}_{A^{\eta}:K}
.
Technically speaking, well-definedness has to be established
simultaneously with its definition (in inductive-recursive fashion),
but we state it as an adequacy lemma below.

1.

\llbracket a:K\rrbracket\eta\,\mathrel{:=}\,X

if
\eta(a)=(A,X)
.

2.

\llbracket\mathbf{a}:K\rrbracket\eta

is the set of well-kinded metaterms
M\in\mathbb{M}

such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}
.

3.

\llbracket(\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,A):K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta\,\mathrel{:=}\,f
,
where
f
is the following function:

\begin{array}[]{l}f:\prod_{B\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{B:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{({\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,A})^{\eta}B:K_{2}}\\
f(B,X)=\llbracket A:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B,X)]\end{array}

4.

\llbracket A\,B:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta\,\mathrel{:=}\,\llbracket A:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta\,(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta)

5.

\llbracket(A\Rightarrow B):\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta

is the set of well-kinded metaterms
M
such that:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(A\Rightarrow B)^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
, and

for every
N\in\llbracket A:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta

we have
M\,N\in\llbracket B:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta
.

6.

\llbracket(\forall{a^{K}}.\,A):\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta

is the set of well-kinded metaterms
M
such that:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}(\forall{a^{K}}.\,A)^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
, and

for every well-kinded type
B\in\mathbb{T}_{K}

and every

X\in\mathcal{VC}_{B:K}

we have

M\,B\in\llbracket A:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B,X)]
.

Lemma C.8 (Adequacy for types).

Let
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family.

1.

Let
\eta
be a candidate-assignment and
A\in\mathbb{T}_{K}
.
Then
\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta\in\mathcal{VC}_{A^{\eta}:K}
.

2.

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
be candidate-assignments and
A\in\mathbb{T}_{K}
.
If
\eta_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta_{2}
then

\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta_{1}=\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta_{2}
.

Proof.

We prove both items simultaneously by induction on
A
.
The second item is straightforward.
To prove the first item:

1.

Variable,
A=a
:
Let
\eta(a)=(B,X)

where we know that
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{B:K}

Then
A^{\eta}=a^{\eta}=B

and by definition

\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket a:K\rrbracket\eta=X\in\mathcal{VC}_{B:K}=\mathcal{VC}_{A^{\eta}:K}
.

2.

Implication,
A=(B\Rightarrow C)
:
Similar to the proof for the second-order case
(
lemma

4.8
).

3.

Universal quantifier,

A=(\forall{a^{K}}.\,B)
:
Similar to the proof for the second-order case
(
lemma

4.8
).

4.

Abstraction,
A=\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,B
with
K=(K_{1}\to K_{2})
:
Then by definition

\llbracket\lambda{a^{K}}.\,B:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket=f
is a function

f:\prod_{C\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{C:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{(\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,B)^{\eta}\,C:K_{2}}

such that

f(C,X)=\llbracket B:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(C,X)]
.
Since
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
is a candidate-family,
to show that

f\in\mathcal{VC}_{(\lambda{a^{K}}.\,B)^{\eta}:K_{1}\to K_{2}}
,
we need to show, first, that the domain and codomain of
f

correspond to

f:\prod_{C\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{C:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{((\lambda{a^{K}}.\,B)^{\eta})\,C:K_{2}}
,
which is indeed the case.
Second, we need to show that
if
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}

and
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{C_{1}:K_{1}}

and
C_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}C_{2}

then
f(C_{1},X)=f(C_{2},X)
.
Indeed,

\llbracket B:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(C_{1},X)]=\llbracket B:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(C_{2},X)]

holds by resorting to the i.h., using the second item in the statement
of this lemma,
noting that

\eta[a\mapsto(C_{1},X)]\mathrel{=_{\beta}}\eta[a\mapsto(C_{2},X)]
.

5.

Application,
A=B\,C
,
where
B\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}\to K_{2}}

and
C\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}}

and
K=K_{2}
:
Then by i.h. we have that

\llbracket B:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta\in\mathcal{VC}_{B^{\eta}:K_{1}\to K_{2}}

and

\llbracket C:K_{1}\rrbracket\eta\in\mathcal{VC}_{C^{\eta}:K_{1}}
.
By the fact that
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
is a candidate-family,
we know that
\llbracket B:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta
is a function

f:\prod_{D\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{D:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{B^{\eta}\,D:K_{2}}
.
In particular,

\llbracket B\,C:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket B:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta(C^{\eta},\llbracket C:K_{1}\rrbracket\eta)\in\mathcal{VC}_{B^{\eta}\,C^{\eta}:K_{2}}=\mathcal{VC}_{(B\,C)^{\eta}:K}
.

∎

Lemma C.9 (Irrelevance).

Let
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family.
If
A:K\in\mathbb{T}

and
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are candidate-assignments that
agree on the free variables of
A
,
then
\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta_{1}=\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta_{2}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
A
.
∎

Lemma C.10 (Substitution lemma).

Let
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family.
Let
A:K\in\mathbb{T}
and
B:J\in\mathbb{T}

and
\Xi(a)=J
.
Moreover, suppose that
\eta
is a candidate-assignment.
Then

\llbracket A\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]
.

Proof.

By induction on
A
:

1.

Same type-variable,
A=a
:
Then
K=J
,
and

\llbracket A\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket a\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket B:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket a:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]=\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]
.

2.

Different type-variable,
A=b\neq a
:
Note that
\Xi(b)=K
,
and let
\eta(b)=(C,X)

where
C\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}

and
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{C:K}
.
Then

\llbracket A\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket b\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket b:K\rrbracket\eta=X=\llbracket b:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]=\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]
.

3.

Eigenvariable,
A=\mathbf{a}
:
Note that
\Xi(\mathbf{a})=K
.
Then

\llbracket A\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket\mathbf{a}\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket\mathbf{a}:K\rrbracket\eta=\{M\in\mathbb{M}\ |\ M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{\mathbf{a}}\}=\llbracket\mathbf{a}:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]=\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]
.

4.

Implication
A=(C\Rightarrow D)
:
Similar to the proof for the second-order case
(
lemma

4.10
).

5.

Universal quantifier
A=(\forall{b^{L}}.\,C)
:
Similar to the proof for the second-order case
(
lemma

4.10
).

6.

Abstraction,
A=\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C

with
K=K_{1}\to K_{2}
:
Assume, by
\alpha
-conversion,
that
b\notin\{a\}\cup\mathsf{fv}(B)

then the left-hand side of the equation is

\llbracket A\{a:=B\}:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket(\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C)\{a:=B\}:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C\{a:=B\}:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta
,
which is a function
f
such that:

\begin{array}[]{l}f:\prod_{D\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{D:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{((\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C\{a:=B\})^{\eta}D):K_{2}}\\
f(D,X)=\llbracket C\{a:=B\}:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[b\mapsto(D,X)]\end{array}

On the other hand,
let
{\eta*}=\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]
.
The right-hand side of the equation is

\llbracket A:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]=\llbracket\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket{\eta*}

is a function
g
such that:

\begin{array}[]{l}g:\prod_{D\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}}}\mathcal{VC}_{D:K_{1}}\to\mathcal{VC}_{((\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C)^{{\eta*}}\,D):K_{2}}\\
g(D,X)=\llbracket C:K_{2}\rrbracket{\eta*}[b\mapsto(D,X)]\end{array}

To show that
f=g
,
note first that they are have the same domain and codomain.
Indeed, it suffices to observe that for each

(\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C\{a:=B\})^{\eta}=(\lambda{b^{K_{1}}}.\,C)^{{\eta*}}
.
To conclude, by functional extensionality, it suffices to show that

f
and
g
are pointwise equal.
So let
D\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}}

and
X\in\mathcal{VC}_{D:K_{1}}
,
and note that:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&f(D,X)\\
&=&\llbracket C\{a:=B\}:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[b\mapsto(D,X)]\\
&=&\llbracket C:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)][b\mapsto(D,X)]&\text{by i.h. }\\
&=&\llbracket C:K_{2}\rrbracket{\eta*}[b\mapsto(D,X)]\\
&=&g(D,X)\end{array}

7.

Application,
A=C\,D

with
C\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}\to K_{2}}

and
D\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}}

and
K=K_{2}
:
Let
{\eta*}=\eta[a\mapsto(B^{\eta},\llbracket B:J\rrbracket\eta)]
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\llbracket A\{a:=B\}:K\rrbracket\eta\\
&=&\llbracket(C\,D)\{a:=B\}:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta\\
&=&\llbracket C\{a:=B\}\,D\{a:=B\}:K_{2}\rrbracket\eta\\
&=&\llbracket C\{a:=B\}:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket\eta(D\{a:=B\}^{\eta},\llbracket D\{a:=B\}:K_{1}\rrbracket\eta)\\
&=&\llbracket C:K_{1}\to K_{2}\rrbracket{\eta*}(D^{{\eta*}},\llbracket D:K_{1}\rrbracket{\eta*})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{applying the i.h. twice}\\
&=&\llbracket C\,D:K_{2}\rrbracket{\eta*}\\
&=&\llbracket A:K\rrbracket{\eta*}\end{array}

∎

Lemma C.11 (Stability under
\mathrel{=_{\beta}}
).

Let
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family.
Let
A_{1},A_{2}:K\in\mathbb{T}

be such that
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}
,
and let
\eta
be a candidate-assignment.
Then
\llbracket A_{1}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket A_{2}:K\rrbracket\eta
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}
.
The interesting case is when there is a
\beta
-reduction or expansion
step at the root.
Indeed, let
B\in\mathbb{T}_{K}

and
C\in\mathbb{T}_{K_{1}}
,
and suppose that
\Xi(a)=K_{1}

and
A_{1}=(\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,B)\,C

and
A_{2}=B\{a:=C\}
.
Then we claim that

\llbracket A_{1}:K\rrbracket\eta=\llbracket A_{2}:K\rrbracket\eta
.
Indeed:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\llbracket A_{1}:K\rrbracket\eta\\
&=&\llbracket((\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,B)\,C):K\rrbracket\eta\\
&=&\llbracket(\lambda{a^{K_{1}}}.\,B):K_{1}\to K\rrbracket\eta(C^{\eta},\llbracket C:K_{1}\rrbracket\eta)\\
&=&\llbracket B:K\rrbracket\eta[a\mapsto(C^{\eta},\llbracket C:K_{1}\rrbracket\eta)]\\
&=&\llbracket B\{a:=C\}:K\rrbracket\eta&\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:hol:substitution}}\\
&=&\llbracket A_{2}:K\rrbracket\eta\end{array}

∎

Definition C.12 (Substitutions and compatibility).

A
substitution
is a function
\gamma

mapping each variable
x

to a well-kinded metaterm
\gamma(x)\in\mathbb{M}
.
If
M\in\mathbb{M}
is a well-kinded metaterm,
we write
M^{\gamma}

for the well-kinded metaterm that results from replacing each occurrence of each
free variable
x
in
M
by
\gamma(x)
, avoiding capture.

If
\eta
is a candidate-assignment,
\Gamma
is a environment,
and
\gamma
is a substitution,
we say that
\gamma
is
compatible
with
\Gamma
under
\eta
,
written
\Gamma\vDash^{\eta}\gamma
,
if for every
x:A\in\Gamma

such that
A\in\mathbb{T}_{K}

we have that
x^{\gamma}\in\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta
.

Lemma C.13 (Adequacy for terms).

Let
(\mathcal{VC}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
be a candidate-family,
and let
\eta
be a candidate-assignment.
Let
\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A
where
A\in\mathbb{T}_{K}

and
\Gamma\vDash^{\eta}\gamma
.
Assume moreover that
\xi
is compatible with the global kind-assignment,
i.e.
\xi\vDash\Xi
(which can always be fulfilled by appropriately
renaming variables).
Then
t^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket A:K\rrbracket\eta
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the judgment
\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A
.
The proof is exactly analogous to the proof in the second-order case,
but now using the higher-order versions of the
irrelevance lemma (
lemma

C.9
),
the substitution lemma (
lemma

C.10
),
and the adequacy lemma for types (
lemma

C.8
).
All rules in the higher-order case follow the same structure reasoning
than in the second order case, except for the conversion rule,
which is new.
In the case of the conversion rule,
let
\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:B

be derived from
\xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A

and
A\mathrel{=_{\beta}}B
.
By i.h.,
t^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket A:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta

so by
lemma

C.11

we conclude that
t^{\eta\gamma}\in\llbracket B:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta
.
∎

The following result constructs one specific candidate-family:

Proposition C.14.

The following indexed family
(\mathsf{Verif}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}
,
defined by induction on the kind, is a candidate-family:

1.

\mathsf{Verif}_{A:\kappa}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\{X_{\kappa}\}

where
X_{\kappa}=\{\diamondsuit\}
.

2.

\mathsf{Verif}_{A:\mathtt{Prop}}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\{X_{A}\}
,
where
X_{A}\,\mathrel{:=}\,\{M\in\mathbb{M}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star\}
.

3.

\mathsf{Verif}_{A:K_{1}\to K_{2}}
is the set
of all functions defined according to
the definition of candidate-families (
definition

C.4
,
item

5
).

Proof.

Let us check that this definition fulfills the
five conditions in
definition

C.4
:

1.

Stability under
\beta
-conversion:
Let
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}

such that
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}
,
and let us show that

\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{1}:K}=\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{2}:K}
.
We proceed by induction on
K
:

1.1

If
K
is a base kind,
then

\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{1}:K}=\{X_{\kappa}\}=\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{2}:K}
.

1.2

If
K=\mathtt{Prop}
,
then it suffices to note that
X_{A_{1}}=X_{A_{2}}

because
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

if and only if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

given that
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}

so
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M

are the same metaterm.

1.3

If
K=(K_{1}\to K_{2})
is a base kind,
then
\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{1}:K_{1}\to K_{2}}
is the set of all functions

f:\prod_{B\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathsf{Verif}_{B:K_{1}}\to\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{1}B:K_{2}}

such that
f(B_{1},X)=f(B_{2},X)
whenever
B_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}B_{2}
.
Similarly,
\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{1}:K_{1}\to K_{2}}
is the set of all functions

g:\prod_{B\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathsf{Verif}_{B:K_{1}}\to\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{2}B:K_{2}}

such that
g(B_{1},X)=g(B_{2},X)
whenever
B_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}B_{2}
.
It suffices to note by i.h. that

\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{1}B:K_{2}}=\mathsf{Verif}_{A_{2}B:K_{2}}
,
so
f
and
g
range over functions of the same codomain.

2.

Inhabited:
Let
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}

and let us construct an element

\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis\in\mathsf{Verif}_{A:K}

in such a way that if
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}

then
\llparenthesis A_{1}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A_{2}\rrparenthesis
.
We proceed by induction on
K
.

2.1

If
K
is a base kind,
K=\kappa
,
then
\mathsf{Verif}_{A:\kappa}=\{X_{\kappa}\}

so it suffices to take

\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis\,\mathrel{:=}\,X_{\kappa}
.

2.2

If
K=\mathtt{Prop}
,
then
\mathsf{Verif}_{A:\kappa}=\{X_{A}\}
,
so it suffices to take

\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis\,\mathrel{:=}\,X_{A}
.
It is easy to observe that if
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}

then
\llparenthesis A_{1}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A_{2}\rrparenthesis
,
as we already observed for stability under
\beta
-conversion.

2.3

If
K=(K_{1}\to K_{2})
,
observe that for every
B\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}

there exists an element

\llparenthesis A\,B\rrparenthesis\in\mathsf{Verif}_{A\,B:K_{2}}

by i.h.. Let:

\begin{array}[]{l}\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis:\prod_{B\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K_{1}}}\mathsf{Verif}_{B:K_{1}}\to\mathsf{Verif}_{AB:K_{2}}\\
\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis(B,X)=\llparenthesis A\,B\rrparenthesis\end{array}

Note that
\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis\in\mathsf{Verif}_{A:K_{1}\to K_{2}}

because if
B_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}B_{2}

and
X\in\mathsf{Verif}_{B_{1}:K_{1}}

then

\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis(B_{1},X)=\llparenthesis A\,B_{1}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A\,B_{2}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis(B_{2},X)

by i.h..
Moreover, note that if
A_{1}\mathrel{=_{\beta}}A_{2}

then
\llparenthesis A_{1}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A_{2}\rrparenthesis

because for every
X\in\mathsf{Verif}_{B:K_{1}}

we have that

\llparenthesis A_{1}\rrparenthesis(B,X)=\llparenthesis A_{1}\,B\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A_{2}\,B\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis A_{2}\rrparenthesis(B,X)

by i.h..

3.

Candidate condition for base kinds:
Immediate by definition.

4.

Candidate condition for propositions:
Let
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{\mathtt{Prop}}
.
Let us check that

X_{A}=\{M\in\mathbb{M}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star\}

has the three required conditions:

4.1

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\in X_{A}

because

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

4.2

If
M\in X_{A}

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.
by definition.

4.3

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be metaterms such that
M_{1}\to_{w}M_{2}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

if and only if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}M_{2}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

because
\to_{w}
is confluent.

5.

Candidate condition for higher-kinded types:
Immediate by definition.

∎

Theorem C.15 (Soundness).

Let
\Gamma,t,A
be such that they have no free type-variables.
If
\Xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\Gamma}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\star
.

Proof.

For each kind
K
suppose that
\mathbf{a}_{K}
is a
eigenvariable such that
\mathbf{a}_{K}\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{K}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}=(\mathsf{Verif}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{T}^{0}}

be the candidate-family of
proposition

C.14
.
Define the following mapping:

\eta(a)\,\mathrel{:=}\,(\mathbf{a}_{K},\llparenthesis\mathbf{a}_{K}\rrparenthesis)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{whenever $\Xi(a)=K$}

where
\llparenthesis\mathbf{a}_{K}\rrparenthesis\in\mathsf{Verif}_{\mathbf{a}_{K}:K}

is given by the construction in
proposition

C.14
.
Observe that
\eta
is a
\mathcal{F}
-candidate-assignment.

Note that
\Gamma\vDash\underline{\Gamma}
,
where
\underline{\Gamma}
is the generative substitution for
\Gamma
,
because if
x:A\in\Gamma
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\maltese}_{A}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star

by correctness (
lemma

C.5
).

Note that the fact that the judgment
\Xi;\Gamma\vdash t:A

holds implies that
\Xi\vdash A:\mathtt{Prop}
,
and moreover
A
is ground by hypothesis,
so
A\in\mathbb{T}^{0}_{\mathtt{Prop}}
.
Then by
lemma

C.13

we have that
t^{\eta\Gamma}\in\llbracket A:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta
,
where moreover
\llbracket A:\mathtt{Prop}\rrbracket\eta\in\mathsf{Verif}_{A^{\eta}:\mathtt{Prop}}

by
lemma

C.8
.
By the definition of the family
\mathcal{F}
in
proposition

C.14
,
this means that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A^{\eta}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t^{\eta\Gamma}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
.
Since
A
and
t
have no free type-variables,

A^{\eta}=A
and
t^{\eta}=t
.
Hence
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle}A{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rangle}t\twoheadrightarrow_{w}\star
,
as required.
∎
